## Co-financer’s contribution means the financial resources required, whether Public Finance or Private Finance, in addition to the GCF contribution (i.e., GCF financial resources requested by the Accredited Entity) to implement the project or programme described in the funding proposal.

There are two types of co-financing: direct and indirect. The former is the use of funds that will be invested in GCF eligible NCD countries, while the latter refers to the use of funds from other sources (e.g., private sector) to finance projects or programmes that differ from the project/programme implementation period.

A.19. Complementarity and coherence

MSLF2 leverages on the success of the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund (LDNF) to complement projects in the AFOLU sector that have positive climate adaptation and mitigation impacts. The LDNF was initiated by the UNCCD, managed by Minova, and funded by GEF. MSLF2 will be implemented as a component under the LDNF. MSLF2 received a grant from the Global Environment Facility (GEF). In addition to the LDNF Fund, MSLF2 has been designed in close collaboration with other projects and programmes at regional and country levels that are targeted by MSLF2 (see Table 7: Appendix I of the Project Document). The project will be implemented in a manner consistent with complementarity and coherence with other projects and programmes in each of the larger countries.

A.20. Executing Entity information

Minova will act as an executing entity for Component 1. Minova will fundraise and deploy an investment vehicle under the laws of Luxembourg named the Minova Sustainable Land Fund (MSLF). MSLF2 will have a Technical Assistance Manager who will act as executing entity for Component 2.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

1. There are close causal links between climate change and both forest loss (including deforestation and forest degradation) and land degradation. Climate-related hazards are already driving both losses today, with projected changes in frequency and intensity of climate related weather events expected to accelerate rates of forest loss and land degradation.
2. Forest loss and land degradation are both major sources of greenhouse gas emissions. The agriculture, forestry and other land use (AFOLU) sector is the second largest source of global greenhouse gas emissions after the energy sector – accounting for around 23% of global emissions. About half of these (5-10 billion tonnes CO2 equivalent per year) are from deforestation and forest degradation.
3. Unsustainable agricultural production practices are a key driver of both forest loss and land degradation. Current efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions in the AFOLU sector have focused almost exclusively on addressing these trends by promoting sustainable forestry and agriculture practices as keys to reverse land degradation, improve soil health and sequester carbon in soils and trees, while also protecting biodiversity and ecosystems.
4. In addition to their impact on GHG emissions, forest loss and land degradation both have negative environmental impacts (including biodiversity loss and reduced availability and quality of water), and socio-economic impacts including poverty reduction and food security.
5. There is an urgent need to scale investment in Sustainable Land Management practices. Estimated climate mitigation costs for the 2018-2030 period are US$47 billion per year, while estimated adaptation budgets are insufficient to meet these needs, particularly in developing countries. The private sector will need to play a key role in financing this investment, but current levels of private sector investment (actual vs required) current levels of private investment remain well below the amounts required.
6. The Miombo Sustainable Land Fund 2 (MSLF2) targets investment in 7 countries across Africa, Asia, and Latin America: Namibia, Costa Rica, DRC, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Malawi, Mozambique, Peru and the Philippines. Component I focuses on forest conservation and restoration while Component II focuses on sustainable land management.
7. Component i provides Technical Assistance to make companies investment-ready, support implementation of their investments in Sustainable Land Management practices (SLM) and monitor progress towards SLM targets.
8. MSLF2 invests in 3 thematic areas: biodiversity, gender, and energy.
9. MSLF2 is a blended finance instrument with a total fund size of US$100 million – including in-kind contributions from the GCF (US$50 million) and private sector partners (anticipated 60% of total fund size). The fund has a strong gender focus, being aligned with the gender action plan for the GCF.
10. Concessional financing from the GCF in the MSF2 program will play an essential role in de-risking projects and creating in-advance access to scale at competitive rates for companies and other developing countries.
11. The Miombo Sustainable Land Fund (MSLF) is a blended finance instrument that provides technical assistance, concessional funding, and social inclusion financing to private sector investments in sustainable land management practices in the Miombo landscape of Southern Africa. The GCF, through its Green Climate Fund (GCF), and the Global Alliance for Clean Cookstoves (GGAC) are key partners in this initiative.
12. The Miombo Sustainable Land Fund (MSLF) is a blended finance instrument that provides technical assistance, concessional funding, and social inclusion financing to private sector investments in sustainable land management practices in the Miombo landscape of Southern Africa. The GCF, through its Green Climate Fund (GCF), and the Global Alliance for Clean Cookstoves (GGAC) are key partners in this initiative.
13. Mitigation Impact: The MSLF program contributes to the mitigation results area on “Forestry and land use”. Over the lifetime of the fund, the expected mitigation impact is approximately 17.2 million MgCO2e in NDCs for countries that adopt the project’s practices.
14. Adaptation Impact: The Miombo Fund program contributes to GCF adaptation results areas on “Health, well-being, food security and livelihoods” and “Climate-resilient ecosystems and natural resources”. The program is expected to yield the following adaptation metrics:
    * 50,000 hectares of restored land and 251,000 indirect beneficiaries with enhanced resilience to climate change impacts (see Annex 23 for details).
    * 105,000 hectares of natural resources brought under improved land emission and/or climate-resilient practice

# B. PROJECT PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Context and Background (max: 1000 words, approximately 5 pages)

### Importance of land and forest resources for human well-being

#### Land and forests are critical to human livelihoods and ecological balance.
According to the FAO, as of 2019, around 1.23 billion people were employed in the world's agrifood systems, and more than three times that figure, or almost half the world's population lives in households linked to agrifood systems. In addition, about 45% of the global land area is used for their livelihoods: 2.36 billion ha in Asia and 940 million ha in Africa.

#### Land degradation is a major driver of climate change and human activities.
Deforestation, desertification, urbanization, industrial development, agricultural expansion, and unsustainable farming practices undermine the ability of the planet's land to provide food, water, and other essential resources. The loss of forest cover has been estimated at 13 million ha per year since 2000.

#### Forest degradation accounts for over 75% of all land use.
The other 30% of lost forests are permanently deforested. Forests cover about 31% of the Earth's land surface (4.6 billion ha) but is the area is shrinking, with an average annual loss rate of around 2 million ha per year.

#### Land degradation and desertification.
Land Degradation reveals that unsustainable land management practices driven by human activities have resulted in severe land degradation with almost 17% of the Earth's land area significantly altered by human activity. The FAO estimates that 35% of global cropland is moderately to severely degraded, while land degradation reduces the productivity of 23% of the global land surface, adversely impacting livelihoods and food security.

#### Land use change.
Land-use change, including deforestation, expansion of agriculture, urbanization, and other Land Use (LU) activities are a major contributor of land degradation, responsible for 28% of global CO₂ emissions and 15% of total greenhouse gas emissions.

#### The economic impact of land degradation is enormous.
3.6 billion people rely on agrifood systems for their livelihoods, among whom 700 million live in households linked to agrifood systems (FAO, 2021). Without healthy ecosystems, those reliant on forests and agriculture could lose significant livelihood opportunities.

- **Food security:** Over the next 30 years, land degradation could reduce global food production by as much as 12%, leading to a 50% increase in food prices. The number of people undernourished is projected to rise from 840 million in 2019 to 1 billion by 2050 and will have to be met by further intensification and expansion of agriculture, which could lead to further land degradation.
- **Population growth:** The world's population is directly affected by land degradation areas where people rely on agricultural activities trapped in a downward spiral of productivity reduction. According to the FAO (2022) State of Food Security and Nutrition in the World 2021, the number of undernourished people has increased from 795 million in 2019 to 840 million in 2020.
- **Food security:** The cost of food production is rising while costs for food are falling. This means reduced food affordability and rising impacts on soil productivity decline.

# Link between climate change and land management/forest resources

18. Climate change and land use are closely interconnected. Land use change and inadequate land management contribute to climate change by releasing stored carbon and reducing the land's ability to sequester CO2. At the same time, the IPCC’s Special Report on Global Warming of 1.5°C (SR15) states that “land degradation is a major driver of climate change and is both affected by climate change and contributes to it.” The IPCC projects (with high confidence) that “climate change can exacerbate existing land degradation problems in many regions, leading to desertification, droughts, heat stress, pests and diseases” leading to further land decline.15

19. Land degradation and the resulting loss of soil organic carbon in turn affect regional and global climate through reduced carbon sequestration capacity. On the global scale, this is driven by changes in emissions or removals of CO2, CH4, and N2O by land biomes. The impacts of these changes on the global climate are summarized in Figure 1 (below). The interconnections between land degradation, climate change, and biodiversity loss are summarized in Figure 2 (below).

Figure 1 Feedback loops between land degradation, climate change, and biodiversity loss

20. The interconnection between climate change and land degradation might have a wide range of impacts across the world, which vary depending on local and regional context. Table 1 below illustrates this link through one example:

| Region | Country | Product |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Africa | Morocco | Highly vulnerable to climate variability and change. Climate and socio-economic environments in semi-arid and arid areas are highly sensitive to changes in rainfall patterns, leading to widespread as well as at least unsustainable agricultural systems, crop failure, and unproductive ranching. |

### Climate change affecting land degradation

Climate change can exacerbate land degradation through increased temperatures, reduced precipitation and desertification. For example, prolonged drought may lead to a loss of vegetation cover, exacerbating land desertification.

### Land degradation affecting climate change

Conversely, inappropriate agricultural practices such as overgrazing and improper irrigation contribute to soil degradation and hinder the land’s ability to adapt to climate change. This mutual influence between climate change and land degradation can have significant impacts on both local and global ecosystem health.


Source: 17296_WB_Morocco Country Profile (WB).pdf (worldbank.org)

## Contribution of the AFOLU sector to global emissions

The IPCC Special Report on Climate Change and Land notes that an estimated 23% of total anthropogenic GHG emissions between 2007-2016 derive from the AFOLU sector. As outlined in Table B below, the AFOLU sector is a significant contributor to GHG emissions in the MSZ F2 NCL countries.

| Country | Reference year | Sector contribution to global emissions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Costa Rica | 2017 | 20.5%<sup>1</sup> |
| Côte d'Ivoire | 2016 | 82%<sup>2</sup> |
| Ghana | 2014 | 34%<sup>3</sup> |

### Barriers to sustainable land management-based adaptation and mitigation

In low-carbon, climate-resilient scenarios, agricultural businesses, smallholder farmers and cooperatives, government institutions, and financial institutions need to plan, invest in the time and human, technical and financial resources required to shift to more sustainable production systems that are less vulnerable to climate impacts. However, there are multiple interconnected barriers that prevent a spontaneous response. These barriers are summarized below.

| Barrier | Interventions to overcome barrier |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Local financing institutions focus on short-term loan-based investments | The MSZF will provide a mix of medium- and long-term loans and equity to local producers to help overcome the barriers associated with the lack of access to credit, high interest rates, and limited availability of financial services. |
| 2. Institutional investors perceive that they cannot make sustainable agricultural and forestry investments | The MSZF offers both a financial and technical solution to the minimum mismatch facing the sector: by matching the investment horizon of institutional investors (Output 1.1), while allowing a more diversified portfolio that includes higher impact investments (Output 1.3). This will be achieved through the establishment of a dedicated fund for Technical Assistance which will significantly decrease the risk profile for institutional and private investors. |

### 3. Difficulty or inability of farmers to adapt their agricultural practices in response to climate change adaptation needs (Sub-Indicator 2.1)

The MSFLF will work closely with existing Technical Assistance (TA) providers to offer a menu of capacity building activities, including training, workshops, and peer-to-peer learning, that are aligned with the LDFN’s approach for developing countries in line with identified localized climate change impacts, to assist in developing and monitoring adaptation strategies. These activities will be designed to build long-term institutional capacity over the longer term. Mirros will partner with an impact enhancement facility to support the development of a gender equality action plan (GEAP) that includes specific targets on G4D and other areas such as the delivery or adoption of gender, social and environmental benefits (Output 2.2).

These TA activities are in line with those implemented for the LDFN and which have proven their effectiveness.

### 4. Gender inequities present obstacles and reduce incentives to adopt climate-smart agricultural and forestry production systems

In September 2022, the LDFN Fund pledged to the 17 Global Criteria with the objective of leveraging this unique global network to engage in peer learning and knowledge exchange on practical insights and lessons learned from the implementation of the fund.

Based on the learnings from the previous fund, the MSFLF will maintain a similar approach and high commitment to gender equality by tracking the gender responsibilities of each investment and their impact. The MSFLF will also build upon the LDFN’s experience in engaging with local communities to foster gender collaborations at Fund and investee level to achieve transformative change.

The MSFLF will continue to work closely with existing TA providers, such as FAO, to provide technical assistance on gender equality issues for investments in agriculture and forestry. The MSFLF will help increase traceability and enhance the ability of producers to engage in international markets by providing them with access to market information, training, and capacity building (Outputs 2.1 and 2.2). MSFLF will facilitate the certification to standards such as Rainforest Alliance and other voluntary services for compliance with national and international laws and regulations (Output 2.1).

For some LDFN investments, international sales for agriculture or timber products were lost due to lack of traceability and/or certification. The MSFLF will ensure that all investments in the sustainable land management sector are certified to internationally recognized standards and certifications as well as other voluntary services, with access to major supply chain buyers and certification bodies which expand MSFLF sourcing opportunities.

Mirva's experience with the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund

31. The design of MSLF2 is directly informed by Mirva’s successful experience and track record with the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund (LDN Fund). The LDN Fund was established in 2015 to mobilize private sector capital for land restoration, generating sustainable land use and ecosystem restoration projects in developing countries. The LDN Fund provides long-term financing of up to 30 years at concessional interest rates to support the development of innovative models of sustainable agriculture and forestry. It targets positive impacts in terms of climate, biodiversity, fights against poverty and hunger.

32. The LDNF employed a tiered structure: leveraging public money to increase private sector investment in sustainable land management projects. The LDN Fund was structured as an umbrella fund with two separate funds: the LDN Fund itself and private institutional investors. The LDNF has supported land restoration projects and programmes by investing in 13 countries, including Costa Rica, Ethiopia, Mexico, Peru, and Vietnam. It has also provided technical assistance to address land degradation and climate change.

As of 2023, approximately 100,000 people (male and female) had directly benefited from seven investments involving 54 projects in the LDN Fund portfolio. The LDNF has been a key driver for scaling up Mirva’s innovative approach to land restoration. Mirva's GHD calculation methodology for LDNF and LDNFS and Annex 23 on the beneficiary calculation methodology.

34. MSLF2 will be designed with lessons learned from the LDNF, including its experience in engaging with the LDN Fund to the design and implementation of MSLF2.

### Contribution to national climate plans and strategies

MSLF2 supports countries to achieve their climate mitigation and adaptation objectives in the AFOLU sector. Appendix 1.6 of Annex 2 – Feasibility Study details the MSLF2’s alignment with national climate and sectoral policy frameworks and regulations as well as the UNFCCC, Paris Agreement, and other relevant international agreements. The Fund will also contribute to NAPs/Programmes as applicable, and contribution to targets key mitigation and adaptation tools such as Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and National Adaptation Plans and Plans of Action (NAPA/NAPAs).

## B (2) (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

The Theory of Change for the MSLF2 describes the causal linkages between the proposed activities as described above, the expected results and the ultimate climate-related goals of the programme. The theory of change is reflected in the following diagram.

The Theory of Change reads as follows: “If the Mirova Sustainable Land Fund II (MSLF2) supports companies focused on sustainable land management (SLM), agroforestry, and/or forest conservation activities that are linked to agricultural and fiber value chains in target countries will generate positive climate mitigation and adaptation benefits. These benefits will be achieved through a combination of: (i) increased adoption of SLM practices by farmers increasing demand while preserving and restoring land biodiversity, and enhancing community livelihoods.” The Theory of Change is supported by the following assumptions:

1. The Mirova Sustainable Land Fund II (MSLF2) will attract institutional investors, including sovereign wealth funds, private equity funds and international investment institutions.
2. Technical and knowledge barriers hindering adoption of SLM practices are removed or reduced through access to markets for sustainable products from regions threatened by deforestation and land forest degradation.

The programme delivers a package of activities aimed at:

- establishing the investment facility,
- fundraising and identifying investment prospects,
- disbursing investments in companies operating in the AFOLU sector that clear due diligence,
- facilitating Technical Assistance (TA) to make companies engaged in SLM activities more “bankable” to MSLF2 and other investors, and
- providing support to enhance the climate and sustainability impact of target companies, generate knowledge and build capacity.

These activity packages will result in four tangible programme outputs linked to the successful performance of the activities described above. These are: (i) increased investment in SLM projects via the investment facility of capital projects delivering mitigation and adaptation benefits in the AFOLU sector; (ii) the track mitigation, adaptation and broader sustainability impacts for example, Mirova could identify a company looking to make the transition from coffee to shade grown (legitimately) coffee production. The switch to shade grown coffee could reduce the impacts of climate change on coffee production and increase carbon sequestration. After the potential investor clear due diligence, Mirova would provide investment via the MSLF2 facility in this company. This investment will be used by the company to transition from conventional coffee production that provides support to improve the producer’s access to markets that are willing to pay a premium for sustainably produced coffee. The investment will also provide technical assistance and capacity building to ensure the project delivers environmental co-benefits, and track and report the project’s climate impact. These outputs will combine to form a virtuous cycle of:

- Increased GHG sequestration as a result of de-risked investments in sustainable forest management and regeneration activities.
- Increased climate resilience of MSLF2 companies, participating communities and ecosystems via the implementation of SLM practices.
- An intermediate outcome is the mobilization of finance at scale to facilitate the achievement of the transformational climate outcomes described above. The programme activities will yield multiple benefits: namely improved biodiversity via the conservation of 20,000 hectares of forested land; increased livelihoods from employing over 13,000 direct workers and gender benefits via the Fund’s alignment with the 2024 2X criteria.”

The MiMoA investment geographic focus strikes a balance between the need to: i) have a deeper understanding of the current state of land degradation in target countries; ii) identify and prioritize potential investment opportunities, projects, etc., to diversify the risk across the portfolio from an economic and regulatory standpoint and iv) mitigate and adaptation strategies for better land use and land cover of the target countries.

### Programme description

45. MiMoA drives climate action along three major paradigm-shifting pathways in the agriculture, forestry, and land use (AFOLU) sector. Targeted subsectors include: (1) planted forests to meet growing demand for timber; (2) agroforestry including annual tree crops with a focus on economic benefits and ecosystem services; and (3) sustainable intensification of existing agricultural systems. The total fund value, and regenerative agriculture (including agroforestry) will account for 60% of the total fund value of MiMoA. This is because these pathways are expected to have the greatest potential in terms of sequestering carbon from the atmosphere and reducing greenhouse gas emissions. These pathways also have the potential to enhance ecosystem services, improve biodiversity, and increase resilience to climate change. The remaining 40% will be allocated to other subsectors that can contribute to land-based adaptation strategies that result from forest and land degradation and create instead a virtuous cycle of increased carbon sequestration.

## B

### Key lessons learned include:

- Geographic scope: The ability to maintain focus across 10+15 emerging countries requires a strong network of local offices.
- Geographic scope: Investment in high-risk countries (regulatory/operational risks and low liquidity) requires a portfolio that includes a mix of projects with different risk profiles, including those in lower-risk countries.
- Sustainable agriculture forestry: A young asset class that still requires derisking through diversification and a conservative and seasonal approach to assessing an opportunity.
- Market volatility: The ability to manage the long-term off-take agreements constitutes a significant mitigant to market volatility. Minerva is strengthening its network with major companies of AFOLU/PGF sectors in Africa, Asia, Latin America, and Europe.

### Component 1 - MSLF Investment Facility (EUR 75M USD 74M equivalent*), Co-financed EUR 260M USD 297M equivalent**

#### Outcome: 1. Finance is mobilized at scale through blended finance structures, enabling the scale up of commercial investments in sustainable land management and agriculture.

- Component 1 involves the mobilization and deployment of finance for sustainable land use practices.
- It employs GCF resources to successfully mobilize finance at scale to transform forest and land uses in agricultural activities. The facility will be structured as a blended finance facility, with a combination of grants, loans, equity, and other financial instruments through which practically the activities are implemented in continuity. Minerva will build on the experience, lessons learned and contacts in existing projects to ensure that the investments are made at scale.
- By providing significant catalytic financing, the GCF will enable MSLF2 to mobilize more private capital at the fund level. The facility will be structured as a blended finance facility with a combination of grants, loans, equity, and other financial instruments through which practically the activities are implemented in continuity.

#### Outcome: 2. The MSLF2 capital is EUR 350 million (USD 371 million*) including a target EUR 15 million (USD 174 million**) of financing for Africa.

- The MSLF2 is mandated to invest in businesses that are committed to the adoption of sustainable land management practices such as agroforestry, reforestation, and soil conservation projects.
- It will finance investments with a total impact of 24.5 MiCOe, 150,000 ha of land under sustainable land practices, and 63,000 direct beneficiaries with enhanced adaptive capacity over the lifetime of the fund.

#### Outcome: 3. The MSLF2 will finance investments in countries where significant mitigation benefits are 17.2 MiCOe, 150,000 ha of land under sustainable land practices, and 63,000 direct beneficiaries with enhanced adaptive capacity over the lifetime of the fund.

# Output 1.1 - Sustainable fund established and capital mobilized EUR 1 million (USD 1.06 million)²

59. Output 1.1 sets the scale of the MLF2 fund vehicle to allow investment by the GCF as well as private investors and development finance institutions where appropriate. This funding, particularly from institutional investors such as pension funds, foundations, and sovereign wealth funds, will enable the MLF2 to operate at scale and is a precondition to the achievement of the Fund's objective.

60. Output 1.1 can be delivered through the following activities:

## Activity 1.1.1 - Funding mobilization

The MLF2 investment vehicle has been established in 2024. Following this initial closing, Miombo’s objective is to raise significant additional capital for the Junior and Senior Shares of the Fund. The GCF will play a catalytic role in the scale-up of Miombo’s efforts by providing USD 1 million (EUR 1.06 million)² as seed funding. This investment will be matched with a size of EUR 350 million (USD 370)³, including both the GCF investment and co-financing.

The fund vehicle will be used to invest in projects that are aligned with Miombo’s strategy for scaling up its activities. These activities ensure private sector actors are aware of the Fund, understand its objectives. The aim is for Miombo to: (a) build a strong track record of successful investments in companies and supply chains that are otherwise challenging to access; (b) have a positive environmental impact and (c) improve perceptions, acts as a catalyst to unlock additional co-investment and derisks these private investments. Miombo’s existing LDCF experience has shown that the Fund can be used to scale up its activities by leveraging co-financing and strengthening Miombo's track record in the natural resource investment space and served as a proof-of-concept that allows Miombo to fundraise for the MLF2 at a larger scale.

The fund vehicle will be managed through due diligence to ensure AML/CFT compliance and alignment with investment objectives. Miombo will negotiate terms and conditions and execute the relevant investment agreement. Infows into the available investment vehicle will be tracked and documented in the audited annual financial accounts for the MLF2.


## Activity 1.1.2 - Fund capital disbursed to projects delivering mitigation and adaptation benefits EUR 349 million (USD 370 million)²

64. Activities under Output 1.2 will disburse funds to projects and companies with potential to generate climate co-benefits. The fund vehicle will be used to invest in projects that are aligned with Miombo’s strategy for scaling up its activities. These activities ensure private sector actors are aware of the Fund, understand its objectives.

The fund vehicle will be managed through due diligence to ensure AML/CFT compliance and alignment with investment objectives. Miombo will negotiate terms and conditions and execute the relevant investment agreement. Infows into the available investment vehicle will be tracked and documented in the audited annual financial accounts for the MLF2.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

### Background

- Emissions reductions related to the sale of such carbon credits will be capped at 20% of the overall fund's impact and deducted from the impacts reported to the GCF.
- Many projects are still in their first development phase and are therefore still generating revenues but have not yet achieved full profitability. They are therefore struggling to finance the expansion of their activities and cannot access funding from commercial banks, which is why it is important for the fund to address this funding gap and to support the diversification of such companies by providing the right financial instruments.
- Mirowa anticipates that ticket sizes for investments made through MSF2 will be in the range of EUR 10 – EUR 20 million. The fund will provide a mix of equity, debt, and hybrid financial instruments. The fund will also consider other financial instruments such as mezzanine financing.

### Assessment

- **Sustainable Land Use**: Implementation of sustainable forestry practices, regenerative agriculture or agro-ecological practices; positive improvement in land cover and productivity; significant reduction of the deforestation pressure.
- **Climate Mitigation**: Carbon sequestration through the above-mentioned improved farming and forestry practices; increased energy efficiency; reduced direct and indirect food loss; increased water use efficiency.
- **Climate Adaptation**: Adoption of climate-smart farming methods such as crop diversification, heat- and drought-resistant crops, water-saving irrigation methods, increase in smallholders' productivity and income, etc.
- **Biodiversity**: At least 10% of the land area under forest cover or at least 10% of the agricultural land and at least 25% of the forest area.

### Compliance

- **Gender equality**: Equal access to land and working conditions; progress towards women's wage earning.
- **Gender commitment**: Gender progress towards the 7Z certification.
- **Compliance with ESG policies**: Companies that qualify for MSF2 investment must also meet the following criteria:
    - Category B1 or B2 of the EU ETS.
    - Compliance with the Fund-level ESG policy and all specific issues policies aligned with Category B1 or B2 (ESG risks).
- **Compliance with international standards**: Such as the IFC Social and Environmental Standards.

### Investment

- MSF2 can be a catalytic role to attract other investors, either at the same time as investment or afterwards.
- The fund will provide additional financing by supporting the investor to find additional funding. Investment from MSF2 may either be provided at the same time as or after the initial investment of the investor. In any case, the total amount invested in each country must not exceed 50% of the total project cost.

### Criteria for Selection

- Each country will have a minimum of one MSF2 country provider is provided in Annex 17.
- The criteria that are indicative only:
    - Mirowa is a commercial investment vehicle and the ultimate funding totals allocated to each country will be determined by Mirowa.
- **The Fund's ability**: To identify a sufficient pipeline of bankable businesses that meet the investment criteria for MSF2 and pass Mirowa's comprehensive due diligence process; and
    - To provide up to EUR 350 million (USD $71 million)**.

### Potential Investment Pipeline

- The potential investment pipeline will comprise high-impact SMES identified through TA delivery partners' candidate pools and/or other sources. The criteria for selection of the pipeline and the diligence processes are described below under Output 1.2; while TA support to investors is described under Component 2 further above.

### MSF2

- MSF2 is open to investment in a wide range of agricultural products.
- This broad focus gives the fund the flexibility to tailor interventions to the priority value chains in different countries, and also to select companies with greatest potential for adapting to climate change.

## Sustainable agriculture
- **Alignment with regenerative agriculture objectives** (providing ecosystem services such as soil carbon retention and carbon sequestration, and resilient farming systems)
- **Application of agroforestry (shade trees, intercropping, and mixed crop-livestock systems)** (using agricultural practices that minimize land disturbance etc.)
- **Ecosystem restoration (restoration of degraded lands and forests)**
  - Reduced soil erosion
  - Increased soil organic carbon
  - Improved water retention and infiltration
  - Provision of ecosystem services such as rainfall interception, Global G.A.B. System

## Sustainable forestry
- **Alignment with sustainable forest management certification** (FSC Forest Management)
- **Application of a landscape approach by intermixing other land uses**, such as crops or livestock in degraded lands and forests zones
- At least 50% of the project area is located on degraded lands, and at least 10% of the project area is located within forest management units
- Strong potential of climate mitigation
- Forest management plan for at least 10 years

### B1
In parallel with the establishment and capitalization of the MSFL2, Mirova will develop the Fund's investment pipeline.
Mirova has an existing active pipeline of potential projects. These projects are ready to go through the MSFL2 investment process.

### B2
Mirova’s deal sourcing and identification team is supported by a strong network of partners and is well connected to actors in the agricultural and forestry sectors. Mirova will source deals for the Fund's investment pipeline through four sources/channels: (i) Mirova’s investment team network, (ii) Natixis group network, (iii) Public and non-profit organizations, and (iv) Private equity firms.

These four channels demonstrate only some of the ways that Mirova will work collaboratively to maximize the efficiency and impact of MSFL2 capital deployment. Mirova has established strategic partnerships with various entities and selected a number of partners to support its deal sourcing efforts. These partners are expected to help facilitate project source funding and fundraising and provide a source of advice and expertise.

Furthermore, several of these products (coffee, cocoa, rubber, and timber) are being supplied by cover crop traceability initiatives that are supported by large buyers. These requirements are likely to increase demand for products which are sustainably produced.

## B

### Cocoa
- **Ivory Coast**: A major driver of deforestation, loss of critical wildlife habitat and biodiversity in West Africa — but also in Latin America and the Caribbean.
- **Demand for cocoa is leading to the expansion of cocoa plantations into areas where it was previously not grown**. Research has found that cocoa cultivation has increased by 10% since 2005, covering an area of about 962,000 hectares (37.4%) of the total cocoa growing area in West Africa and Central America.
- **Cocoa can be grown in ways that promote biodiversity and support health**. Cocoa is a shade-tolerant tree crop that can be grown agroforestry systems with other crops and trees. Environmental benefits of this approach include improved soil health, nitrogen fixation, water regulation, reduced erosion, increased carbon sequestration, increased biodiversity. Agroforestry cocoa production also provides additional income and improving food security.

### Nuts
- **Examples based on the most common varieties in Africa**:
  - **Almonds**: Morocco
  - **Cashew**: Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Nigeria

A study on cashew production in West Africa found that cashew is grown mainly in agroforestry systems, with trees providing shade and potentially substantially reduce GHG emissions while also providing income to farmers**.

### Financial Times (18th August 2023): Have we reached peak coffee?
- **Vietnam, India, Côte d'Ivoire and Benin are the largest producers of Arabica coffee production. A study found that there have been significant shifts since even moderate increases in temperature. The study also concluded that the area of land suitable for the major producing countries will largely shrink in the coming decades**.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

## Timber Malaysia
- Between 2015 - 2019 forestry was the second highest driver of deforestation (approximately 26%).
- The forest sector is responsible for ~75% of the GHG emissions from land use change.
- The demand for timber is set to triple by 2050.
- The industry is vulnerable to climate change.

## Rubber Malaysia, Calab, Province, the Philippines
- The global land area used for rubber production has increased over decades. This expansion is accelerating and will continue in the future.
- Climate change is already impacting rubber production through more frequent and more variable weather patterns.
- Average rubber production is 28°C, which is projected to increase well above this level by 2050 and likely have a negative impact on yields.

## Citrus Morocco
- Some of the fruit trees are vulnerable due to climate change in terms of production, productivity, yield, and the safety of irrigation water.

**Page 26**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

## Avocados Peru
- **Avocado production has substantial and irrevocable environmental costs and risks** due to its high demand for the fruit, leading to a climate change scenario where yields are projected to decline by up to 50%.
- **Climate change is expected to reduce avocado production in Latin America**, with extreme weather conditions, extensive soil erosion, and increased pest pressure posing significant threats. The report suggests that by 2030, depending on how the climate changes, yields could be reduced by 10%, 40%, or even 65%.
- **Similarly, as avocado production increases in response to rising demand**, water availability is becoming a critical issue due to heat stress and droughts. This is particularly concerning for countries where water resources are already limited.

## Bananas Costa Rica, The Philippines
- A study on banana yields in 27 countries found that the world's largest producer – Brazil – has seen its yield decline since 1961 due to changing climate conditions.
- **The report highlights significant reductions or disappearances of banana production** in several countries. For instance, in the Philippines, the study suggests that the country could lose up to 50% of its current banana production by 2030.

85. Detailed descriptions of each step in the investment screening process can be found in the Programme Description document in Annex 2 – Feasibility Study. The main steps of the process are summarised below:

#### Screening Committee
87. In cases where preliminary analysis is positive, the Investment Team will gather more information on the proposed project and assess its potential to deliver concrete adaptation and social benefits and/or string investment bankable, while also considering the MFSL2 TAF’s ability to deliver concrete adaptation and social benefits and/or string investment bankability, but lacks the required ESG technical assistance. The Investment Team will then present this information to the Screening Committee for further consideration.

88. This step also supports the identification of a potential investor need and eligibility for technical assistance. Where the project is deemed eligible for MFSL2 TAF support, the MFSL2 TAF investee demonstrates alignment with MFSL2 result and impact areas, strong potential to deliver concrete adaptation and social benefits and/or string investment bankability, but lacks the required ESG technical assistance. The Investment Team will then present this information to the Screening Committee for further consideration.

89. Where the project is deemed eligible for MFSL2 TAF support, the MFSL2 TAF Manager will recommend the prospective project receives technical assistance from the TAF for investment readiness and/or string investment bankability (described under Output 2.0 provided by MFSL2).

90. The Screening Committee will assess the prospective company’s eligibility, need and readiness for investment without investment readiness TA support. The MFSL2 Investment Team will allocate a dedicated team for further implementation of the project.

#### In depth analysis and investment decision process
91. Upon approval of the opportunity by the screening committee, the Investment Team undertakes an In-depth Analysis (IDA) of the proposed project, organises meetings with company and potential co-financers to further structure the deal, and provides a detailed financial model of the transaction.

92. The IDA will also assess the prospective investment’s expected positive impacts (including climate impacts) and approach to risk management.

The opportunity is presented to the investment committee 1, which provides a go/no decision.

MSFLF will offer debt, mezzanine and equity investments in companies that are implementing climate resilient Sustainable Land Management practices with a significant environmental impact and poised for growth. Mezzanine investments are intended for companies in the growth phase with an established operational track record and planning on scaling-up high-impact projects. Senior secured debt or debentures will be offered to companies with a strong credit rating.

The TAF will provide tailored support to investees implementing improved production processes, monitoring, and reporting of emissions and sequestration, the development of a Gender Action Plan (GAP), and ESATs and other tools. After a positive screening, TA delivery partners will provide investment readiness to help the investee meet the Miura2F Fund’s eligibility criteria for investment readiness. ##### The type of activities that will be eligible for investment readiness support include:

* Developing an investment readiness plan that will include the following: identifying potential project concepts and concept development; identifying relevant indicators/metrics as reflected by the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs); assessing the investee’s current technical capacity and financial resources; developing a business case for their proposed activities; strengthening internal control systems and management capabilities. Designing investment management systems and internal controls tailored to their business. Design interventions to build on existing strengths, address weaknesses, and improve performance. Developing an implementation plan that will strengthen financial reporting and analysis. Assess feasibility of technological solutions to streamline their operations and inform decision making.

Tasks under this activity include supporting investees to implement streamlined systems of Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) for greenhouse gas emissions reductions, climate resilience and social inclusion, as well as alignment with corporate sustainability reporting initiatives and the UN Sustainable Development Goals.

The design process was based on the successes and lessons learned from Mirena II Land Degradation Neutrality Fund. The design process included a review of the LDNF’s geographical focus, revenue streams, deal size and other features.

A key consideration in the design of MSLF2 is the need to ensure that the project portfolio has a strong focus on agriculture, forestry and land use sectors, and a careful consideration of potential alternative approaches. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 38

## Alternative B: No action - Mirova and Minerva launch a national fund

### Reason for rejection:

No-action: Mirova and Minerva launch a national fund.

Agriculture and forestry businesses in developing countries unable to overcome barriers to climate action without assistance. No paradigm shift from business as usual (BAU) is required, as the proposed fund would be a new approach.

Grants and TA support without the MSME facility investment

Grant financing is insufficient to meet global and national adaptation and mitigation targets. The proposed fund does not address market failures or technical problems (monetary cannot risk crowding out private sector investment).

Investment facility without concessional blended finance and TA

Risk/return mismatch limits institutional investor appetite with de-risking from junior investors. The proposed fund is scale-limited and cannot invest in higher impact projects (see Annex 3).

Single-country / more limited country focus

Less diversification of risk investment. Higher up-country transaction costs means less access to capital for developing countries.

Single-instrument facility focused on loans

Debt instruments best suited to stable, lower-risk investments. Therefore, inability to invest in newer, higher-growth sectors that are essential for sustainable development.

Single-instrument facility focused on equity investments

Significant elevation of higher risk investments would require higher rates of return, potentially limiting the universe of candidate companies or else failing to reach sufficient fund size.

## B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Countries need private capital to support climate action

148. The Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2 targets investment in sustainable land management within 11 countries including 1 Least Developed Countries and African nations. In each of these countries, unsustainable agricultural production and other land uses are a major source of greenhouse gas emissions, and thus have significant negative impacts on climate change, as well as biodiversity loss.

149. The Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2 aims to support sustainable land management within their national climate agendas; implementation of these objectives has been challenging. In the countries and regions targeted by the MSLF2, the finance for sustainable land management is often constrained due to high debt servicing requirements. In some instances, public finances are severely constrained due to high debt servicing requirements.

## B

### Microprojects anticipate a total size for MSLF2 of EUR 350 million (USD 371 million) and a ceiling of EUR 500 million (USD 550 million). The program anticipates co-financing GCF between EUR 289 million (USD 325 million) to EUR 438 million (USD 464 million), yielding a co-financing GCF ratio between 3.8 and 5.8 for MSLF2.

### Junior equity from GCF will make this investment profile possible. Junior players play a key role in reducing risk for private investors, as they are often the first to invest in projects. This model is also attractive because it involves the involvement of private investors who otherwise might have been too risk-averse to participate. The first loss financing is intended to be provided by the GCF, which will provide concessional funding for the development and implementation of land use projects in developing countries. Rather than subsidizing private profit, this model is bringing additionality. The target risk-adjusted return on investment (RAROI) for junior equity investors is below market expectations.

### MSLF2 employs the correct financial instruments for effective downstream investment, with minimum concessionality.

## Without GCF commitment, Microprojects does not expect to reach the target fund size and lower/midterm investments would have to be made, implying reduced climate impact potential. The GCF commitment will impact the portfolio composition by allowing MSLF 2 to raise more risk (and generate higher expected return).


---
**Page 36**
---

### B. Without blended finance approach, the private sector’s appetite to invest in agricultural and forestry projects in emerging markets may be limited due to various factors:

- **High Risk:** Agricultural and forestry projects in emerging markets often face significant risks, including market volatility, political instability, and social challenges.
- **Limited Returns:** Agricultural and forestry investments in emerging markets offer lower returns compared to other sectors. Private investors are more likely to invest where they can expect higher returns or opportunities in other sectors.
- **Lack of Access to Capital:** Many agricultural and forestry projects in emerging markets require significant upfront capital investment, which private investors may be reluctant to provide on their own. Without access to additional funding sources, such as concessionary or public financing, private investors may be unable or unwilling to fully finance these projects.
- **Long Payback Periods:** Agricultural and forestry projects often have long payback periods, especially those focused on development of new planned areas or value chain improvements. Private investors may be reluctant to commit to such long-term investments.

### 1.6. In the absence of blended finance, these factors may deter private sector investment in agricultural and forestry projects in emerging markets, limiting the potential for sustainable development and economic growth in the sector. Blended finance can help address these challenges by providing additional funding sources that are more attractive to private investors and incentivize private sector investment in agricultural initiatives. Based on the experience of LDN Fund and MSFL2 funding models, blended finance can be used to support the development of new planned areas or value chain improvements for agricultural and forestry projects in emerging markets without a derisking mechanism. Private investors can find the same level of expected returns as they would from other sectors.

### As illustrated in the financial analysis (see Annex 3), GCF commitment would also enable a riskier geographical exposure for MSFL2, which would otherwise focus on the most developed emerging countries. GCF commitment enhances MSFL2 impact and sustainability by enabling the project to invest in new planned areas or value chain improvements. This investment strategy would have reduced exposure to smaller or more liquid markets, where exits might prove challenging.

### B.6 Exit Strategy (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

#### Exit Strategy

The project will have a closed-end structure, with a term of 12 years to be counted from its inception and potentially to be extended for two successive periods of one year. Most of the investments will be made through self-liquidating instruments (e.g., equity).

The exit strategy for each portfolio company will be determined by Moxiva’s investment team. For equity investments, the exit options are analysed before the investment during the due diligence process. The sales shares can usually be sold to a third party or to another shareholder. Equity investments in the project may include favourable exit clauses as well as alignment clauses between shareholders and first refusal clauses to facilitate the transfer of assets beyond preferred terms.

### Sustainability and replicability

The MSFL2 will build on the success of the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund (LDN Fund), which has been a pioneer and a leading example in addressing land degradation, climate change, biodiversity loss, and environmental challenges. The LDN Fund has supported land restoration projects and programmes by investing in 13 countries since its inception in 2014. The LDN Fund has provided innovative solutions to address the critical issue of land degradation and climate change. A “test-and-learn” approach was applied to the LDN Fund, which has been approved for a second phase with an additional USD 50 million on the MSFL2.

The ambition of the MSFL2 is to be the next step in scale as agricultural and forestry funds that demonstrate strong commitment to Sustainable Land Management practices. This approach works. The LDN established the precedent with a junior tranche comprising 20% of that fund’s capital. GCF established (through Component I) MSFL2

levels between c.280 million (USD 297 million) in co-finance, with a hard cap for the fund of EUR 500 million (USD 530 million). With the fund at this size, the MSLF2 will be able to invest at sufficient scale that actual and perceived market demand is met. This will enable the fund to more confidently invest in businesses that integrate sustainable land management practices beyond the lifetime of the fund.

166. The MSLF2 is designed to sustainably overcome several overlapping barriers in developing country hydrological landscapes, including those related to climate change. This sets the stage for a "new normal" where producers have technical knowledge and networks, the ability to source inputs for sustainable production, and access to credit and markets. These changes will help countries adapt to climate change while also helping them to change agriculture paradigms shifting pathways related to three of the GCF results areas: agriculture and food security, forests and land degradation.

167. The MSLF2 strategy for sustainability, replicability and scale-up involves ensuring that the above-mentioned barriers have been overcome in a way that is financially sustainable. This will be achieved by targeting investments that prioritize sustainable sourcing. MSLF2 investors will benefit from increasing global demand for sustainably produced food and fiber products. The MSLF2 TAF will also provide technical assistance to producers, including those that make sustainable production a viable and attractive option, de-risks producer businesses and helps them provide market-ready products. This will help ensure the long-term viability of the investments made by the fund and further strengthens the programme's potential for sustainability and replication. Additionally, the MSLF2 TAF will provide support to investors in assessing their investment readiness, operational performance, and enhance the climate, social and environmental impact of their activities.

168. The MSLF2’s successful operation and exit will make it easier for Mirco and other fund-managers to raise funds that support sustainable land management investments. This will help ensure a steady supply of capital from investors available for climate action in this sector. In addition, investors who have gained experience with the MSLF2 may potentially have increased appetite for funds in adjacent sectors.

#### 171. As noted in appendix 1 of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2), the land-use and forestry sectors are significant contributors to GHG emissions in Mozambique's largest carbon sources, even as climate change threatens the continued productivity of these sectors. The Mzimba Sustainable Land Fund will help target countries achieve their mitigation commitments under the Paris Agreement by supporting a range of activities that promote sustainable, regenerative agricultural practices, strengthen forestry, agroforestry and conservation agriculture will contribute to increased resilience in Mozambique's largest carbon sources while also contributing to adaptation to climate change.

#### 172. MSLF investments in the sustainable agriculture sector will improve soil health and fertility and enhance water retention and carbon sequestration. This will result in several additional benefits for the beneficiaries and increase income from agricultural production, which is a major source of livelihoods and employment as well as an important source of revenues for smallholder farmers. In addition, the promotion of heat- and drought-tolerant crops will reduce the pressure on water resources and improve food security.

#### 173. The Mzimba Sustainable Land Fund will also contribute to the resilience of the landscape. Agroforestry projects will increase sequestration in perennial biomass and reduce emissions due to soil disturbance. Projects will improve climate resilience by reducing soil erosion, increase soil organic carbon content, and enhance water retention. These projects will also support best-in-class commercial reforestation/afforestation projects that employ a landscape mosaic approach and the use of native tree species. The Mzimba Sustainable Land Fund will further contribute to a reduction in value chains' direct and indirect carbon footprints through improved practices for post-harvest handling, processing, packaging and transport.

#### 174. This section is based on the information provided in Annexes 20 and Annex 23 (beneficiary calculation methodology) for further details on the MSLFZ climate impact potential.

#### 174. The MSLF is designed to sustainably overcome several overlapping barriers to climate action in the development country agriculture, forestry and land use sector. Under business-as-usual, companies are locked into a cycle of deforestation, degradation, and loss of biodiversity, which leads to increased greenhouse gas emissions, reduced climate resilience, and more climate-related risks.

The MSLF2 programme's paradigm shift sets the stage for a "new normal" in agriculture, forestry and land use. It will provide access to sustainable inputs for productive cultivation, and access to capital with a cost of risk that make adoption of these practices economically viable.

#### 153. At the market level, Mirova will provide a proof of concept via MSLF2 to change investors' perception about the business case for land use investments in developing countries. The first vintage of MSLF was launched into the sustainable energy sector, which was seen as a high risk & decade ago and is now considered mainstream.

The Fund will be structured as a series of vintages, each with an increasing size and complexity, to attract increasingly mainstream investors. As the market gradually matures and demonstrates success, new investors and complex projects will be able to enter the market. The first vintage was focused on degraded land, which is increasingly mainstream investment. This paradigm shift could turn sustainable land use investments into a mainstream asset class.

#### 154. The second vintage of MSLF2 will focus on degraded land in developing countries. Restoring degraded land is the only viable option for the world's growing population to sustain itself. It just requires new thinking and financial tools to empower the poor, creating a good track record that can be replicated by others.

#### 156. The first vintage of MSLF2 was focused on degraded land in developing countries. Land use market, while specific projects can be replicated. MSLF2 aims to select best-in-class models across various geographies and land use sectors. Models that demonstrate robust financial performance and positive ESG outcomes will be prioritized for investment.

#### 157. MSLF2 will be the second of several vintages, increasing in size. It is designed to be bigger than the first LDN Fund (LDNF) and will have a greater proportion of senior investors. The second vintage is expected to attract more investors to the successive vintages. Future vintages are expected to have a progressively lower junior capital buffer, with a higher proportion of senior capital. This will allow for larger projects and investments that have a greater percentage of senior investors, increasing the level of private investment in sustainable development.

**MSLF2's key contribution to SDGs 13 includes the removal and/or sequestration of 17.2 MCO2e, through the implementation of climate resilient and soil restorative practices by producers.**

* **Restoring land and forest restoration and conservation:** The MSFL2 will support the sustainable use of land resources, which supports a number of key animal and plant species that are essential for terrestrial food production.

**181. Environmental co-benefits: Further to the environmental co-benefits investments under MSLF2 will include conditional covenants to the lending agreements relating to the preservation of natural cover and conservation areas. A minimum target of 10% of agricultural land and 25% in forested areas in the total portfolio will need to be subject to these conditions, which will be monitored by the Fund's environmental monitoring system. The project Biodiversity Plans will also include specific measures to protect endangered species and native flora and fauna.**

**182. Social co-benefits:** The MSFL2 supports long-term quality employment and sustainable livelihoods for farming communities in NQI countries, with a particular focus on women and youth. This is achieved through the provision of training with a special attention to seasonal and daily workers throughout the employment cycle (validated by third-party certification bodies). The MSFL2 will also provide support to farmers who are interested in becoming Rainforest Alliance certified, with a covenant to close pay gaps within 3 years. As a result of these measures, it is expected that approximately 10,500 smallholder farmers and their families will benefit from livelihoods, as well as 7,000 direct workers contracted under the Fund's lifetime in NQI countries.

**183. Economic co-benefits:** The MSFL2 provides support to access to finance for microenterprises through assistance with the T4A module under Component 2; the share of sustainably produced foods and fibres will be increased, which will contribute to support access to higher income markets through certification support. This will generate significant economic benefits.

**184. Gender-sensitive development impacts:** The MSFL2 programme will also contribute to the achievement of SDG5 by providing gender-sensitive financial services and technical assistance in line with the Gender and Social Inclusion Plan in alignment with the 2x Standard. As detailed in Annex 8 - Gender Analysis and Action Plans (GAAPs), the MSFL2 will provide targeted support for women farmers, who are often barred from participating fully in agricultural activities due to gender-based barriers preventing them from fully partake and benefit from agriculture and land-use investments. The fundamental objective of the GAAP is to ensure that women have equal access to resources, information, and equal opportunities for leadership at all levels of decision-making in political, economic and public life by tracking the proportion of women in managerial positions.

D.A. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

In each of the 7 GCF-eligible countries targeted for MFLSF investment, the agriculture, forestry and land use sector accounts for a significant share of employment in rural areas and is often the largest source of employment. In most cases, it is also one of the biggest finance needs of the agricultural sector to employment and GDP, and climate finance needs for the AFOLU sector in each country. Climate change is already having an impact on the productivity of the AFOLU sector. One-third of the target countries are in Africa, a region with particularly high exposure to climate risks. The vulnerability of communities living in these areas depends largely on their access to and use of climate-resilient land and water practices, the vulnerability of farmers and communities who depend on the AFOLU sector for their livelihoods, and the ability of governments to provide adequate support. As noted in Annex 8 (Gender Assessment and Action Plan), the impacts of climate change are not gender neutral. Many poor people, especially women, girls, and indigenous peoples, depend heavily on the natural resources for their livelihoods and food security. Climate change is likely to exacerbate existing inequalities and land change. Almost 80% of employed women in least-developed countries rely on agriculture as their primary livelihood activity. Women are also more vulnerable than men to climate change impacts such as drought, land degradation and diseases.* The literature on this topic in fact illustrates a strong link between gender and land because these are closely tied to gender-based roles in lands rights, access to resources and incentives, opportunities to participate in decision making, and the distribution of costs/benefits of projects targeting land improvement.

Absence of alternative sources of financing (e.g., fiscal or balance of payments gap) that prevents government from addressing the needs of its population and environment.

198. Estimated climate finance needs for each of the regions targeted by MILF2 investment are consistent with high Country National Determined Contributions and National Adaptation Plans emphasize the need for increased investment in sustainable land management practices to address climate change impacts, including increasing resilience and adaptation. In the countries and regions targeted by the MILF2, the climate finance gap is significantly above the resources available from public sources (e.g., GCF, World Bank, etc.). The private sector is currently constrained due to high debt servicing requirements. Based on debt sustainability analysis by the World Bank and IMF, it can be concluded that the countries targeted for investment under MILF2 (i.e., Ethiopia, Kenya, and Ghana) are already in debt distress. Given the size of the investment gap and the finite resources of both domestic public and private sector investors, a significant portion of funding for the transition to sustainable land use practices will need to come from the private sector.

197. However, private investment in sustainable land management practices in each of the target countries remains constrained by several factors. First, there is a lack of trust between private investors and governments. Private investors assign a risk premium to the target countries; this premium is even higher for the ACP sector, given its vulnerability to extreme weather and other factors. As noted in the barrier analysis (see Section B 1), implementing sustainable land management practices requires significant upfront investment that may not be recovered by commercial lenders focused on short-term cash flow financing rather than the longer-term revenue required to transition to more sustainable land use.

196. Given the limited availability of public finance and the limited appetite of private investors to finance the transition to Sustainable Land Management practices, funds such as MILF2 are important tools to create transformational change in these countries. The GCF is a key partner for MILF2 because it has the ability to provide funds’ activities, as the concessional capital provided by GCF is key to securing co-investment in the fund, and ensuring that private sector actors require less risk premium.

195. In each of the 7 countries, investments made by MILF2 will contribute to transforming land use practices to more sustainable methods, by financing agricultural production models that preserve and restore ecosystems and water resources, as well as by supporting the development of new markets for sustainably produced goods. This also demonstrates the financial viability of investing in sustainable land management practices, thus leading to current perceived and actual risks and laying the groundwork for investment at scale (particularly from the private sector).

194. Investments made by MILF2 will demonstrate both the financial viability and positive climate and environmental benefits of sustainable land management practices. This will lay the groundwork for investment at scale in SLM projects across countries and regions that the fund is targeting.

Need for environment regulations and policies

193. The increased emphasis on international supply chain traceability highlights the importance of professionalizing the operations of producers and farmers in the agriculture, forest and land use sectors in developing countries. While there are a number of initiatives to support this process (e.g., GEF SLM), these initiatives lack the institutional capacity to integrate these approaches into companies’ standard business practices. This leads to lost revenue opportunities for smallholder farmers and other producers. The proposed investment under MILF2, through the TA Facility will provide support to portfolio companies and build their capacity—to meet the requirements of international supply chain traceability. This will also allow them to better understand their impact on critical environmental and social impacts (see Section B 3 of this funding proposal).

### D

#### Engagement with NDCs and other key stakeholders

190. Annex II of Arre 2 – Feasibility Study details the MSFLF’s alignment with national climate and sectoral policy frameworks and regulations for each of the 7 target GCF countries. This in-depth analysis includes the programme design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting framework as well as mitigation and adaptation tools such as Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and National Adaptation Plans and Plans at Action (NAP/NAPAs). Although disparities exist among target countries in terms of their institutional capacity to engage with NDCs and NAP/NAPAs, the MSFLF is designed to ensure that all countries deploy adequate pathways and commitments to facilitate the deployment of MSFLF in support of AFCD.

#### Engagement with NDCs and other key stakeholders

191. Mirow has had continuous engagement and dialogue with MoEs since the beginning of programme development. This engagement was formalized through the adoption of the Concept Note and Funding Proposal preparation process. Mirow has conducted outreach via email and teleconference to share information about the MSFLF, including a webinar series on the MSFLF design and structure in February 2018 and Non-Objection Letters (NOLs). The list of participating countries will be finalized prior to the targeted Board Meeting publication outcome. In addition to NOD, key stakeholders (governmental and non-governmental) were invited to participate in a series of webinars held from November 2017 to February 2018. The first series of webinars presented the proposed programme and fund structure and sought initial feedback and comments on the design. A second series of webinars was held in March 2018, with a third series being held in English, French, and Spanish between February and March 2018. In addition, Mirow has published the MSFLF programme document and funding proposal for public comment. The comments received were logged into a tracker and the input used to continuously refine the programme design. This detail can be found in Appendix A of the Funding Proposal.

#### Implementation phase

194. During the implementation phase, country ownership will be ensured at both fund and investment level via ongoing engagement and dialogue with relevant stakeholders. At the fund level, a stakeholder engagement plan has been developed to ensure that all key stakeholders are engaged throughout the project cycle in terms of activities and responsibilities. Key stakeholders for this ongoing engagement at fund level include TA delivery partners, country representatives, and other relevant ministries. The MSFLF will also engage with national supply chain actors. At the investment level, country ownership includes engagement with local businesses to ensure that they are aware of the programme’s goals and objectives. Mirow will provide TA support as needed on the development and implementation of a stakeholder engagement plan that identifies key stakeholders, vulnerable groups as appropriate, and describes processes for establishing meaningful and informed engagement at both fund and investment level.

#### Commitment

195. Mirow is committed to ensuring that investments made through MSFLF are aligned in context with possible future NDCs and NAP/NAPAs, where relevant. Country MoEs recognize the importance of maintaining regular communication with National Designated Authorities (and other relevant ministries) in each of the target countries.

- **Mirow** will share annual updates on the investment portfolio in each country, and investment readiness and implementation progress.
- **Mirow** will monitor each country’s published climate policy and programme documents to ensure continued alignment between MSFLF investments with new projects and initiatives. Mirow invites the NDA to keep it informed of new developments.
- **Mirow** will participate in calls requested by the NDA to share updates and perspectives.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 45

## 200. Micro Fund (EUR 15 million USD 79 million) from GCF to help the MSFLF reach efficient scale and accelerate climate action in the agriculture and land use sector. Of this total EUR 70 million (USD 74 million)** is a new commitment by the GCF, with the balance of EUR 30 million (USD 25 million)** coming from the MSFLF Facility. Beyond the mechanistic increase in fund size, a significant junior commitment by the GCF will have a strong catalytic effect for markets with a target junior tranche of 10% of total committed capital. The GCF has committed EUR 350 million (USD 371 million)** and a ceiling of EUR 500 million (USD 532 million)**. Total capping for commitment to the MSFLF is EUR 850 million (USD 904 million)**, with a target fund size of EUR 500 million (USD 532 million)**, yielding a cofinancing ratio between 3:1 and 5:1. As a result of GCF support, MSFLF is expected to yield GBP 687 million (USD 749 million) in climate finance for the agriculture sector, with an additional GBP 102 million (USD 111 million) for land use, representing a total of GBP 789 million (USD 860 million) in climate finance. For downstream investments MSFLF will normally be expected to have a minimum co-financing ratio of 3:1, with the GCF providing up to EUR 250 million (USD 274 million)**.

### Adequacy of financial structure to address objectives

The objective is to keep the junior ratio at a minimum level possible as it is a scarce resource and senior capital is easier to raise for MSFLF which has already demonstrated its capacity to scale up. The GCF commitment will be used to demonstrate that the fund can be scaled up, and to prove the viability of the asset class by launching successive funds with progressively reduced junior shares.

### Structural constraints

The structural constraints (SC) have nevertheless challenged this minimum level of 15%, requiring a higher protection level. MSFLF may also need to integrate constraints from some junior investors that will be required to be exposed to the portfolio.

### In terms of scale, the target junior funding is therefore represent c.20% of MSFLF at final close even if the target of MSFLF is to cover 15% (fiction) and the worst case scenario would be at 25%. (cosh). Such caps reflect the constraints from some investors that will be required to be exposed to the portfolio.

### Why a junior tranche?

A junior tranche percentage would imply a significant change of the investment strategy. At the same time, a senior investor commitment is needed for the fund to be viable and to have a minimum level of 15% of total committed capital by GCF support. A sub-scale fund would experience less diversification, less economies of scale, and less ability to cover costs. The junior tranche will also provide a buffer against market volatility, protecting investors' money versus other sources. Therefore, GCF investment in the senior tranche is a vote of confidence that MSFLF can generate attractive returns for commercial investors.

### Additional considerations

The GCF commitment will be used to demonstrate that the MSFLF can reach long term debt-like instruments and equity investments. Equity investment targets companies at an earlier development stage but already have a track record in terms of financial performance. The junior tranche is intended for companies in the growth phase with an established operational track record and planning on scaling up. The GCF commitment will be used to demonstrate that the MSFLF can generate returns for commercial investors.

### Equity investment from GCF

Equity investment from GCF will make this investment profile possible, as equity money is a scarce resource. In addition, the GCF commitment will allow the MSFLF to reach its target size faster and with less investments than would be required if the GCF commitment does not expect to reach the target fund size and less investments have to be made, implying reduced climate impacts. The GCF commitment will impact the portfolio composition by allowing MSFLF to take more risk.
