**(and expected return). In the absence of GCF investment (and a substantial junior tranche), investors will not agree to finance the riskiest projects, which are also the most impactful. Deploying the fund through more risky investments would be counterproductive and could even result in negative returns.**

By providing equity (i.e., lower-risk financing) that is concessional, MSLF2 will be closing to the equity gap in developing countries, where a scarcer financial-resource than debt. Strengthening equity plays a crucial role in increasing the attractiveness of projects for private investors and can also attract other sources of finance such as multilateral development banks or international banks, and consequently expand their activity (e.g., increasing the area under sustainable management). This is particularly relevant to smallholder farmers who are often excluded from the formal financial system. These farmers usually plan a higher number of new hectares of land under sustainable productive management (vs. traditional agriculture).

MSLF2 will have other debt-like instruments, rather than the ones provided by local banks or more mainstream development finance institutions. The main difference is that MSLF2 will provide longer-term funding (maturity up to 15 years) and will be able to finance larger projects (up to 30 million tonnes CO2e). This means that it will only short term funding, mostly to finance working capital (<1 year). MSLF2 will offer longer tenor (maturity up to 15 years) and will be able to finance larger projects (up to 30 million tonnes CO2e). This means that it will not be able to finance the processing part of the value chain by building a unit.

As MS LF2 is a concessional instrument, it also enables a richer geographical exposure for MS LF2, which will otherwise focus on the most developed emerging countries. At least developed countries are expected to have a lower cost of capital than developing countries and thus be less attractive to private investors.

**Minimum concessionality:** The MSLF2 operates on the principle of minimum concessionality so that it facilitates the sustainable long-term transformation of commercial finance for sustainable agriculture and forestry, currently dominated by high-cost debt. This is achieved through two main mechanisms: (i) a 10% net return threshold; and (ii) an attractive risk profile. The latter enables MSLF2 to be more attractive than below market expectations. Several potential investors have indicated that they would expect more than 10% net return on their investments in order to consider them as "attractive" for private investors, while others have indicated that they would only invest if the instrument is "attractive" for private investors. Instead, it contributes to offer then greater downside protection in case my portfolio underperforms.

**Cost-effectiveness:** Based on experience and an anti-emission reduction estimates (see Annex 2), a fund size of EUR100 million will be able to finance projects with an estimated cost-effectiveness ratio of 3.5 tonnes CO2e per USD invested, which is below market expectations. The cost-effectiveness ratio for the MSLF2 is 4.5 tonnes CO2e removed and sequestered, for an indicative mitigation cost of EUR140/tonne USD (ICOS). This means that the fund will be able to finance projects with a cost-effectiveness ratio of 3.5 tonnes CO2e per USD invested, which is below market expectations.

The cost-effectiveness ratio for the MSLF2 is 4.5 tonnes CO2e removed and sequestered, for an indicative mitigation cost of EUR140/tonne USD (ICOS). This means that the fund will be able to finance projects with a cost-effectiveness ratio of 3.5 tonnes CO2e per USD invested, which is below market expectations.

World Bank is also the market price for verified emission reductions in the carbon markets. This same investment would have been worth approximately EUR140/tonne USD (ICOS) or USD89/tonne USD (USD2.33/tonne USD 2.47$/tonne).

**MSLF2 will also be a cost-effective instrument by leveraging existing resources and commitments of TA partners across its global network to identify target investments and support those businesses to become more attractive for private investors, which in turn increases the overall impact of the fund and enhances complementarity and coherence with existing project preparation support facilities.

## Activity 2.2.3 Programme level monitoring and evaluation
* **Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

### Monitoring and reporting system and procedures
211. In its role as Accredited Entity and through the MSFL2 as Executive Entity, Mivos will oversee and supervise the implementation of the MSFL2 programme in accordance with the AMA signed between Mivos and the GCF, and consistent with the IRMF. Mivos will be responsible for fund level monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of the project, including the development of a MEL framework, and will work closely with the country team, implementing the necessary tools and methods to facilitate monitoring and evaluation of the project.
212. The programme will be monitored at least annually by Mivos, in collaboration with the GCF, and country NDA during the implementation of the programme via annual supervision missions (or more as needed) that will include results, reflection and planning meetings (in-person or virtual), and other activities to ensure alignment within the framework of regular monitoring and evaluation (M&E) procedures established implemented during the programme’s inception phase.
213. The EEM will procure M&E and knowledge management specialists to support the monitoring and learning (MEL) of investments at the fund level, including the development of a MEL framework for the MSFL2 portfolio. The EEM will also work closely with the GCF, country team, and country NDA and Mivos. The procured M&E specialists will work with the MSFL2 Investment Team and MSFL2 to develop MEL tools and approaches to deliver reporting arrangements and monitor KPIs described below.

**Page 62**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 59

## Reporting arrangements

214. At the portfolio level, Mirova will produce the following monitoring and reporting products for the MSFLF:
- Annual audited financial statements — to be communicated no later than 30 April each year
- Quarterly reports on the Fund’s performance including the NAV of the Fund on those dates, as well as an overview of the portfolio
- Impact report — to be communicated no later than 30 June each year. The annual impact report provides details on performance of the Fund in comparison to KPIs at the fund level and environmental/social measures from the managing companies and from the targeted Fund.

## Evaluation processes

215. The ESG Team and the MSFLF Investment Team will be jointly responsible for coordinating the independent interim and final evaluations:
- The interim evaluation will be conducted using a question-driven approach, and may include assessments against the criteria of relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, and sustainability.
- The interim evaluation will be instrumental in contributing — through operational and strategic recommendations — to improve implementation, setting out any necessary corrective and adaptive management measures for the remaining period of the investment, and identifying relevant lessons learned.

217. The final evaluation will assess the relevance of the intervention at the fund level, its overall performance, as well as sustainability and scalability of results in relation to the ESG objectives and targets set by the Fund’s platforms.
- Both evaluations will contribute to the evidence base for sustainable agriculture and forestry production at a national level and across the target regions, including through the development of new policy platforms. The mid-term evaluation will be undertaken when delivery reaches 50% of the initial total budget and/or mid-point of scheduled programme duration. The independent Final Evaluation will be launched within six months prior to the actual completion date of the programme.

## Environmental and Social KPIs

220. Together with the investees, the E&S specialists will conduct the following monitoring and reporting activities related to ESG and positive impact:
- Review and report quarterly on the E&S Action Plan (ESAP) progress
- Provide regular updates at the investor’s level to review and advise on ESG milestones
- Report annually on ESG impact metrics
- Report on service outputs

Additional information on the incidents to be reported, environmental and social KPIs can be found in Annex 6 – E&S, and also Annex 11 – M&E Plan.

## E. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### F1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

#### Selected Risk Factor 1
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Low  
**Impact:** Medium  

*Description:* Agronomic/technical underperformance of the downstream investments. The impact of downstream investments depends on effective implementation of technical and agronomic interventions. There is a risk that these interventions are not implemented effectively or yield results below expectations.

Based on experience with the LDN Fund, a strong technical/agronomic due diligence will be conducted prior to investments. The Mirros team has developed over the years a network of third-party experts to facilitate such due diligence and provide advice on how to improve agronomic/technical performance in specific contexts (e.g., country), and will rely on proven sustainable land management interventions. Additional support will be provided to investee companies through the Climate Assistance facility on a case-by-case basis.

#### Selected Risk Factor 2
**Category:** Governance  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Medium  

*Description:* Local governments are not committed to slowing land degradation and deforestation, therefore sustainable change cannot be achieved. This risk may also apply where local governments facilitate investments in sustainable land management conflict with other laws or established interests.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The Mirros team will engage in proactive dialogue with local government officials as a key element of the due diligence process. The MuLTF will evaluate the degree to which investment businesses have achieved buy-in from local governments and their stakeholders. The required stakeholder engagement process (see Annex 7) ensures technical support for the development of appropriate land management practices, policies, programmes, and interventions at the national level that can facilitate local government buy-in and support.

#### Selected Risk Factor
**Category:** Other  
**Probability:** Low  
**Impact:** Medium  

*Description:* Emissions risk – an investor project fails to achieve its GHG target because either: A. Anthropogenic or natural disasters; B. Project design flaws, implementation, and/or

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 61

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Invests monitor forest and land areas for conservation and restoration, monitoring and case-by-case reporting on the effectiveness of investments in these areas. The GCF does not expect that this mitigation measure will make a material contribution to the GHG impact of MSLF2 investments; and this trend is expected to continue for MFLF2. Reversals are accounted for in the KPI Framework, considering the large areas of protected forest and extensive land use change in developing countries. The GCF has developed a methodology to account for potential forecast emissions impacts across reversals and be built on conservative input assumptions, so the actual sequestration should be higher than that reported (see Annex 22). This will tend to counter any reductions in sequestration.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cost | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Risk of capital loss for investors. MSLF2 projects are subject to various project risks that may result in capital losses for investors, including but not limited to: (i) the risk that the fund cannot achieve effective impact on climate change; and (ii) the risk that the fund can reach effective action to support low-emission and climate-resilient agriculture, forestry and land use projects in developing countries. The overall risk profile of the GCF’s capital is relatively high risk in case of underperformance or poor performance compared with peers.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Diversification across sectors, companies, and geographies will help to mitigate MSLF2 portfolio risk. Mirva has an established track record in investments in East Africa, South Asia, Latin America, Asia and Latin America. To date, Mirva has over EUR 31 billion in assets under management (as at 30 September 2024), strong sector expertise, and a diverse portfolio of companies across sectors including agriculture, forestry, energy, water, and infrastructure. Mirva will monitor and manage investments throughout the life of the fund to identify and mitigate performance risk and environmental risks as well as environmental and social impact.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Inability to find investment opportunities and deploy capital in a timely manner. Implementation delays could potentially reduce the period over which the fund generates climate benefits and investment returns, lowering its overall impact.

While many newly formed investment funds struggle to develop their investment pipeline, Mirva’s experience with the LDF Fund and strong track record. Over the past years, Mirva has assembled a strong team of professionals in the field of sustainable investments (approximately 100 employees) and more than 400 opportunities). The team brings a broad international sourcing capacity with demonstrated transaction execution capabilities. Mirva’s TA facility partners will also support the development of investment pipeline and developed a strong network with various stakeholders including development finance institutions, civil society organizations, and other investors. Mirva has also been involved in several initiatives related to the AFOLU sector. In addition, Mirva’s TA facility partners will further support the identification of potential investments opportunities for MSLF2. The team is currently working on a number of investment opportunities in Nairobi, which will allow the team to even have a more pro-active approach. Several investment opportunities in the target countries are already under active consideration for MSLF2.

### Selected Risk Factor 6

## Description
Inability to "crowd in" sufficient complementary investment to reach effective size. The ability to deploy capital for sustainable forest management activities depends on MLSF's success in mobilizing investment from public and private sources.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- MLSF3 benefits from Mirva's track record of successful projects, combined with the downside protection provided by the junior tracker will overcome the perceived and actual risk associated with investing in a new fund. The combination of Mirva's strong track record and sustainable supply chain sourcing has increased demand for private sector investors to gain exposure to high impact sustainable land and forest management investment opportunities.
- Mirva has already developed a strong pipeline of projects that are consistent with any GCF maximum equity stake in MLSF3 will be respected.

## Selected Risk Factor 7
### Category | Probability | Impact
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Difficult to properly monitor the performance of the investments. Investors' companies are primarily responsible for reporting relevant data that is used to track and report climate impacts from the programme. Late or inaccurate reporting can lead to a lack of transparency, which may impact the ability of investors and other stakeholders to monitor and manage performance and report to the GCF and other investors in a timely manner.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Leveraging on the experience of the LUN fund, Mirva has developed a strong monitoring framework to follow its investments. This framework will be used to track and report climate impacts from the programme.
- Through TA and investment teams, Mirva will play an important role in providing additional value to the complex risk management of the local Mirva offices in Africa, Asia and Latin America and also play a key role to ensure closer monitoring.

## Selected Risk Factor 8
### Category | Probability | Impact
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Adverse conditions arising from possible "force majeure" changes in the business environment (mass riots, civil war etc.)

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Portfolio diversification will limit exposure to a given country and the country selection for MLSF3 programme has taken into account a country risk assessment. Mirva's Country Risk Department is consulted for every investment appraisal.
- Political risk insurance policies can be purchased in case of political risks, but this would be at the discretion of the investor.
- The MLSF3 focus on businesses that directly benefit the local community and engage actively with stakeholders helps those businesses to better anticipate and potentially mitigate this risk, as it aligns incentives with local stakeholders to avoid disruption.

## Selected Risk Factor 9
### Category | Probability | Impact
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cost | Medium | Medium |

### Description


---
**Page 66**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 63

## Product price volatility and cyclicality of agriculture and forest product prices impact borrowers' ability to meet repayment obligations.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

MSLF2 will engage in active portfolio diversification, limiting exposure to one type or only a few specific products. Companies will be supported to obtain sustainability certifications that help their products attract a market premium. Given the long investment period for MSLF2, we will smooth out short-term price fluctuations.

## Selected Risk Factor

### Category: Force
- **Probability:** Low
- **Impact:** Medium

### Description:

MSLF2 is a Euro fund and therefore may be exposed to currency risk where products and invested returns are priced in local currency or Dollars. 

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Financing in local currency will be limited, with investments made in Euros or in Euros directly. To minimize the FX risk at investee level, MSLF2 will focus on companies active in supply chains with a significant exposure on international markets, therefore getting revenues from exports in USD or EUR.

## Selected Risk Factor

### Category: MLFT
- **Probability:** Low
- **Impact:** Medium

### Description:

Risk that the Fund is misused for money laundering (ML) and terrorist financing ("TF") or any other financial misconduct or crime. 

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The AE has an AML/CFT policy and dedicated compliance team members aiming to ensure that ML/TF risks in the process of fundraising for, and investments by, the Fund are properly identified, monitored, mitigated, and reported. In order to mitigate ML/TF risk, MSLF2 will not invest in any project or activity where the provision of funds could be used for terrorist financing or any other financial misconduct or crime. No individual or entity that is listed on any Security Council Sanctions list, including the UN Consolidated Sanctions list, will be involved in any manner with the project or its activities, either as a direct participant or indirectly through an intermediary. MSLF2 expects its partners and expect its business partners to apply similar standards for conduct when working for and serving the Fund.

## Selected Risk Factor

### Category: Other
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Medium

### Description:

Risk that MSLF2 is not able to set a transaction according to original expectation. 

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Most of MSLF2 investments will be debt instruments with no exit risk, as the equity exposure is capped at 30%. The repayment obligations are clear and well-defined in the legal documentation. Specific rights (such as call along, drag along clauses) will be included in the legal documentation to facilitate investment liquidity. Exit opportunities might involve: i) strategic investors (corporate/financial); ii) secondary market participants; iii) other MSLF2 investors; iv) liquidation of investments and repayments.

We are seeing a growing interest from these different players for the sectors targeted by MSLF2. Assessment of the exit strategy will be done on an ongoing basis.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk classification

The proposed MSF2 programme is expected to generate positive environmental and social impacts as it enables the implementation of sustainable agriculture and forestry practices in a manner that respects human rights, cultural heritage, and biodiversity.

### 0.1 Environmental and Social Risk Classification

#### 0.1.1 Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS)

The ESMS has been designed in accordance with Good International Industry Practice principles (GIPPI) and the GCF’s ESMS requirements. The overall programme rating according to ESG is categorized as I-2 and sub-projects as B.

#### 0.1.2 Environmental and Social Management System

It provides a framework defining roles and responsibilities, quantitative and qualitative objectives, policies, procedures, processes, tools, and resources for the management of environmental and social risks and impacts throughout the strategic control and management of the organization’s ESG risks and responsibilities through:

- Developing action plans and responses to manage and mitigate significant risks
- Facilitating continuous improvement in achieving ESG performance

#### 0.1.3 Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA)

The ESIA and impact assessment process is fully aligned with the common approach across the Natural Capital Business Unit at Mirova and in compliance with the GCF’s ESIA requirements.

### 0.2 Environmental Risks

Environmental risks cover potential negative impacts on the natural environment – both in terms of protection of biodiversity, ecosystems, and water resources, as well as the conservation of cultural heritage and other natural resources. Social risks cover potential negative impacts on local communities, project stakeholders and the wider society, including human rights, health & safety at work, corruption and bribery, land tenure and use change, indigenous peoples’ rights, stakeholder engagement and grievance management.

### 0.3 The topics typically covered by the ESG risk assessment comprise the following:

- Business Ethics
- Legal Frameworks & ES compliance
- Certifications and Standards
- Environmental Impact Assessment and Management
- Pollution Control: Energy and Water use
- Biodiversity Conservation
- Health & Safety Procedures
- Health & Safety at work
- Corruption, Bribery and Security
- Land Tenure and Land Use Change
- Indigenous Peoples’ Rights
- Stakeholder Engagement and Grievance Management
- Cultural Heritage

### 0.4 Categorizing projects based on their level of inherent risk is also a best practice implemented by all development financiers in accordance with the Equator Principles.

The risk categories, adapted from IFC risk categorization, are used to classify project level inherent risk. Only if the assessment during due diligence highlights inherent risks that cannot be mitigated through the ESMS and/or ESIA, will the project be classified as Category A projects, which generate unmitigable negative impacts. MSF2 will only invest in projects rated as “positive impact” or “no significant impact.” Projects with a rating of “negative” or “negligible” impact are excluded at this stage.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 65

## Green Climate Fund Regulation Proposal

European SFDR regulation, covering all three aspects of positive contribution: DNGH (Do Not Significantly Harm) and good governance. It ensures a robust integration of sustainability risks in all investment processes and limits the potential negative impact of these investments.

### Alignment with the Fund's Impact Strategy

Alignment is required for investments presented in Annex 6 and Annex 11 are monitored at two levels:

- At the level of the invested assets, we use them as parameters in the investment decision process and as a basis for monitoring and reporting on alignment.
- At the level of the fund, we monitor the alignment and performance of invested assets with respect to the ambitions of a given fund.

After reviewing the potential positive impacts of a project potential, MFSL assesses its alignment with the Fund's impact strategy, including Fund-level impact indicators and co-benefit indicators as described in Section 1.2.3. The assessment is based on the information provided by the applicant in accordance with Annex 4 in general terms, and by sector and type of investment. Regular monitoring and review arrangements of ESG risks are established at both levels.

### Information Disclosure

In accordance with GCF information disclosure requirements, the Environmental and Social (E&S) risk management framework for investments is published on the Fund's website and the Mirvoya web site as of 27 January 2021 at latest. Physical copies will be made available in each country as investments are made.

## Gender lens investing thesis for MFSL

The MFSL builds on Mirvoya’s philosophy and dedication to responsible investing, based on the notion that driving long-term economic growth and creating better environments, social and governance (ESG) practices is essential to creating long-term value for society. The MFSL strategy is built around three pillars:

1. **The Fundamentals of the MFSL Strategy:** To invest in sustainable land-use projects and companies in the forestry sector with a focus on climate change mitigation and adaptation as well as co-benefits in biodiversity protection, community inclusion and gender equality.
2. **Programmatic approach to gender equality:**
   - The MFSL’s investment criteria are established on a foundation of identifying, monitoring and mitigating social and gender risks, underpinned by sound and accountable governance. In alignment with the ESG risk management framework for investments, the MFSL will:
     - Comply with national environmental, social and labour laws and regulations.
     - Comply with the Mirvoya’s ESG Policy which includes any ESG requirements of investors in the fund) and all issue-specific Policies.
     - Meet the objectives of internationally recognized sustainability standards such as the IFC Social and Environmental Performance Standards.
   - Eligible investments must be land-based projects that fulfill and demonstrate several eligibility criteria, including:
     - Demonstrated benefits: projects should clearly benefit local communities (job creation and smallholder support).
     - Environmental & Social (E&S) risk management robust ESG standards are an integral part of the Fund’s investment process.

### G.3. Financial management and procurement

#### Financial Management

**Financial Implementation Arrangements:** The document describes the contractual relationship between Mirova as AE, the MSF2, the TA Facility (TA), and project investee companies. It details provisions regarding financial reporting requirements for each party involved in the investment process.

- **Mirova's Role:**
  - Reporting to the GCF on behalf of the MSF2.
  - Submitting reports on amounts committed by the MSF2, broken down by country and project.

  - Providing a provisional disbursement schedule on an annual rolling horizon.

- **Project Investee Companies:**
  - Reporting to Mirova as part of the GCF reporting line.
  - Submitting reports on disbursements from funds allocated for each investment, including any money-laundering and other evaluations according to respective institutions' internal policy requirements. These reports will be submitted in English to the GCF.

## Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme documents are subject to redaction only if they contain specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

- No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF; no information is being provided in confidence.
- If confidential information is included (e.g., decisions made within the funding proposal), including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - full copy for consideration and publication on the GCF website.
  - redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Secretariat’s assessment of the PSAA applicant

The Secretariat has assessed the document titled “PSA accreditation: application and review checklist” submitted by Mirova, société anonyme (referred to as “Mirova” or “the applicant”). The assessment includes an overall capacity assessment and recommendations for GCF project-specific approach (PSA) accreditation. The following is a summary of the assessment.

| Overall capacity | High |
| --- | --- |
| Fiduciary functions | Basic fiduciary standards<br>Specialised fiduciary standard for project management<br>Specialised fiduciary standards on for-lending and/or blending (for loans, equity and guarantees) |

| Conditions | Not applicable |

The Secretariat has augmented its capacity by using external experts to undertake the applicant capacity assessment review. A summary of the assessment is presented in this document.

### Applicant capacity assessment summary report

#### Introduction and institutional assessment
- Mirova is an investment management firm specializing in sustainable finance, managing over EUR 31 billion across various strategies that focus on environmental and social (ES) impacts.
- Its extensive experience in managing funds in areas such as renewable energy, natural capital, agriculture, and infrastructure has led to a strong equity investments portfolio. Mirova serves as a well-established firm in the sustainable finance field.

- Mirova is owned by Natixis Investment Managers, itself fully owned by Group BPCE (France’s largest banking group). The Group has an extensive international reach.
- The applicant has received B Corporation certification and multiple


---
**Page 90**
---

# Methodology

## The accreditation process for Mirova began with the submission of the first set of materials to GCF, based on a customized application form and materials requested.

An external review team conducted a comprehensive review, assessing the applicant's policies, procedures, and track record against GCF accreditation standards. 

The review included an assessment of internal controls, procedures, capacities, track record, human resources, complemented by interviews with key Mirova personnel and reference checks. The review also involved discussions with Mirova’s project and prospective MLF2 investors. Key informants from Mirova covered areas such as general management and financial administration, procurement, project management, E&G, and gender equality.

The review team found that the information provided further evidence of Mirova's internal capacities and facilitated a walk-through of key systems and tools. The review also confirmed Mirova’s compliance with GCF’s capacity, ethical adherence, and the high skill level of its employees.

## The second stage of the review concluded with a final assessment and review of the risks based on the information provided in May 2021.

Mirova was assessed in accordance with several GCF policies and standards relevant to accreditation, including but not limited to:

- “Guidelines for the Plan for the GCF 2024–2027” (GSP Decision B.26/13);
- “Matters related to the accreditation framework” regarding the accreditation process (Decisions B.24/15 and B.26/13);
- “Guidelines for the selection criteria for accrediting national, regional, and international implementing entities and intermediaries, including the Fund’s fiduciary principles and standards and environmental and social safeguards” (Decision B.07/02);
- “Policy on Anti-Corruption” (decision B.22/19);
- “Anti-Money Laundering and Countering the Financing of Terrorism Policy” (AML/CFT Policy) (decision B.18/10);
- “Policy on the Protection of Whistleblowers and Witnesses” (decision B.SM-2018/21);
- “Governance and Social Management System: Environmental and Social Policy” (Decision B.19/18);
- “Comprehensive Information Disclosure Policy of the Fund” regarding the disclosure of ESG-related information (Decision B.23/25);
- “Updated Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan 2020–2023” (decision B.24/12).

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP263

## Proposal name: Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2 (MSLF2)

### Accredited entity: Mirova

### Project/programme size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### I. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview

The programme "Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2 (MSLF2)" intends to facilitate private-sector investment in sustainable land management (SLM) practices across eleven countries: Ghana, Peru, Morocco, Malaysia, Costa Rica, Philippines, Côte d'Ivoire, Indonesia, Kenya, Thailand and Uganda. The Mirova Sustainable Land Fund is a joint initiative between the World Bank and ITAP.

The programme aims to create agribiod and fibre value chains in target countries that deliver significant climate mitigation (up to 10 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent per year) through SLM practices under the GCF project-specific assessment approach, which enables the GCF Secretariat to convene a panel of independent experts to assess the project's overall financial viability and suitability for GCF financing. The review by the ITAP assumes that the Secretariat’s assessment of the operational readiness is satisfactory.

Available scientific evidence demonstrates that climate change is intricately linked to forest loss, including deforestation and forest degradation, as well as land degradation. Climate change will exacerbate these processes through increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events projected to exacerbate rates of forest and land degradation in many regions. Forests are also a major source of terrestrial carbon sequestration and constitute significantly to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, making the AVCDI sector the second-largest global source of anthropogenic GHG emissions after energy production. Approximately half of these emissions stem from deforestation and forest degradation, emphasizing the crucial need to address unsustainable agricultural practices. Sustainable forestry and agriculture practices can help mitigate climate change by reducing GHG emissions while improving resilience and reversing land degradation. Beyond their impact on GHG emissions, forest loss and land degradation also have far-reaching environmental and socioeconomic


---
**Page 96**
---

consequences, including biodiversity loss, reduced water availability and quality, and threats to livelihoods, food security, and public health.

Addressing these challenges demands urgent and scaled investment in SLRM practices. All MSIP2 target countries share significant vulnerabilities to the impacts of climate change, with many experiencing increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, as well as prolonged droughts. These climate-related hazards exacerbate deforestation, land degradation, desertification, and biodiversity loss, leading to food insecurity for millions of people and communities. Unsustainable agricultural practices, including land clearing for monoculture plantations, contribute significantly to greenhouse gas emissions and soil erosion, further diminishing their capacity to sequester carbon and increasing vulnerability to climate extremes.

While the target countries (including those for which the AE has not yet obtained an NOL) have diverse ecosystems and climates, they are all vulnerable to these drivers. Examples include Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, and Indonesia, where face deforestation and land degradation due to large-scale palm oil production, a major export product industry that is crucial for their economies but carry significant environmental costs. Kenya and Myanmar also face similar challenges with coffee and tea plantations, respectively, which are both highly productive yet environmentally costly.

Coastal countries such as Costa Rica, the Philippines and Thailand are especially at risk from extreme weather events like hurricanes and typhoons. In addition to deforestation, these forests have also disrupted livelihoods and economies dependent on agriculture and tourism.


SLRM investment strategies targeting carbon removal. The proposed programme submitted by the accredited entity (AE) consists of a blended finance initiative focusing on targeted SLRM investments in non-NOL countries. This will be achieved through direct investments into eligible companies via an investment facility with technical assistance (TA) aimed at making these companies more sustainable and resilient to climate change, while also reducing their greenhouse gas emissions and social impact reporting in areas such as climate mitigation, biodiversity and gender equality. The programme is expected to generate approximately 10 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent avoided or removed per year through investments in SLRM activities, including afforestation and reforestation, and 60 per cent to regenerative agriculture; including agroforestry, while maintaining the land use for food production.

The fund's strategy emphasizes sustainability and scalability by reducing risks, promoting sustainable business models, and encouraging long-term partnerships with the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and other development partners. The programme also aims to build a strong network of stakeholders through its membership Council, fostering stronger links between portfolio companies and supply chain buyers committed to sustainable sourcing. These effects are expected to create positive feedback loops that enhance the sustainability of these investments not only for the targeted businesses but also employees, communities and the environment.


Under the terms of the proposal, the AE requests EUR 80 million from GCF to facilitate and support the implementation of SLRM activities in the target countries. In terms of financial instruments, the AE is requesting EUR 70 million in the form of equity and EUR $5 million in the form of debt. The proposed programme will be implemented through a single fund that will facilitate SLRM investments amounting to a total of approximately EUR 359 million, of which EUR 300 million would come from GCF resources and EUR 59 million from other sources. This is the entire fund that will include investments in non-NOL countries funded by proceeds from investors and/or third-party contributions. The AE proposes that the AE will provide portfolio companies in the target countries will also trigger additional co-investment from these portfolio companies and other third-party investors.


*Generally understood as a system of farming practices and processes aimed at improving and restoring land health, biodiversity, water quality, soil fertility, and reducing GHG emissions. It goes beyond sustainable agriculture by actively working to regenerate the land and ecosystems.
*See https://www.2xchallenge.org/ (accessed 2 January 2025).


---
**Page 97**
---

# Impact potential

**Scale: Medium**

The funding proposal highlights a range of anticipated outcomes resulting from the proposed programme across various GCF mitigation results areas (MRAs) and adaptation results areas (ARAs), reflecting the investment scope of the proposed programme. Specifically, the headline impact is summarised as follows:

- **Outcome 1:** GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/requested, with a total expected removal of 17.4 million t CO2e (core indicator 1, MRA 4).
- **Outcome 2:** Increased food security and improved livelihoods, including gender considerations, with beneficiaries adopting improved and/or new climate-resilient livelihoods (core indicator 2, MAA 3, Supplementary 2.1).
- **Outcome 3:** Hectares of natural resources brought under improved low-emission land management practices, with 50% of the target area in each core indicator (core indicator 4, ARAs 2 and 4.1).
- **Outcome 4:** Hectares of terrestrial, terrestrial non-forest, freshwater, and coastal marine ecosystems brought under restoration and/or improved management, with 70,000 hectares targeted (core indicator 4, ARAs 2 supplementary 4.1).

The programme is also expected to empower enabling positive environmental outcomes, contributing towards the achievement of the GCF’s strategic objectives on SLM investments (core indicator 6).


---
**Page 98**
---

indicator7), technology deploymentanddissemination(core indicator6), and effective knowledge generation and sharing (core indicator4).

The AE/EE expects to accomplish these outcomes via the following outputs:

(a) Output 1.1: A scalable blended finance fund vehicle is created and capitalized with concessional funding from GCF, World Bank, and other donors. This will enable large-scale financing of sustainable agriculture, forestry and land management projects.

(b) Output 1.2: Investment capital is deployed to businesses implementing SL practices, including through the AE/EE’s TA fund. The majority of investments will be on projects that deliver significant climate adaptation and mitigation benefits.

(c) Output 2.1: Tailored TA is provided to prepare business for investment. Activities include capacity building, project development support, technical assistance and financial and administrative processes to enhance eligibility for financing.

(d) Output 2.2: Portfolio companies receive support to implement strategies for improved climate, biodiversity, and social outcomes. This includes developing and executing plans for GCF-funded projects and other private sector investments.

(e) Output 2.3: Lessons learned and best practices from portfolio companies are documented and shared to inspire broader adoption of sustainable practices. Partnerships with relevant stakeholders will be established to scale up solutions within target regions.

The AE/EE’s proposal’s logical framework is coherent and aligns well with the objectives of mobilizing investment, fostering SL practices, and delivering climate, environmental and social benefits. However, it also relies on numerous assumptions, particularly concerning the fund composition and the ability of the AE/EE to generate a sufficiently high-quality pipeline within the four-year investment period of the fund. The funding proposal notes that the AE has already developed an active investment pipeline for the fund, but does not provide sufficient detail regarding this pipeline or the due diligence process. The AE has provided further details regarding its pipeline building work as part of the application, including a summary of the projects and companies within the fund’s initial investments. The AE however has not provided much detail that would enable the GCF to assess whether the pipeline is sufficiently robust to meet the proposed targets. Highlights are that the pipeline will be expanded through various channels, including the AE's investment team, collaborations with public and non-profit organizations, and networks of supply-chain


---
**Page 99**
---

actors and off-takers, however, the ITAF anticipates that building a robust pipeline for MSUF2 may encounter challenges given the market opportunity, targeted climate outcomes, geographical scope, and the characteristics of the companies and investments involved:

(a) Targeted geographies: Many of the target countries, while potentially rich in opportunities, face significant barriers such as weak institutional frameworks, regulatory environments, and limited access to reliable financial markets. These factors could complicate project development and execution.

(b) Investment team: The ITAF will be supported by an investment team that is mostly Europe-based. This may result in the fact that some of the most attractive opportunities are outside Europe, which could limit the number of any investments (no requirement is included for a particular portfolio allocation).

(c) Size of targeted companies: The focus on small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in the target countries will be limited to those with revenues below EUR 150 million. These businesses ready to absorb the average investment size of EUR 15 million. Many SMEs may lack the resources or capacity for a detailed climate risk assessment.

(d) Alignment with climate and sustainability goals: Identifying projects that not only meet financial viability thresholds but also deliver substantial climate adaptation, mitigation, and ecosystem services benefits is challenging due to the complexity of these objectives. This dual emphasis may narrow the pipeline, not least since the AF will delegate TAF implementation to a third party. The choice of methodologies could underpin the potential of the TAF to build a high-quality investment pipeline.

15 Portfolio selection and climate impact assessment: Assessing the potential of investments in terms of their contribution to meeting national mitigation and climate adaptation outcomes in emerging economies is complex. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) 2018, Climate Change and Land, an IPCC Special Report on climate change and land use, highlights that the assessment of carbon sequestration potential through carbon sequestration in soils and biomass, while also enhancing adaptation, for many projects is challenging. This is due to a lack of reliable data, which typically arise from variability in social and ecological conditions, limited baseline data, and a lack of robust measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) and monitoring and evaluation systems. The assessment of the climate impact of investments requires an understanding of local capacities, further complicating selection of investment projects as well as implementation and monitoring frameworks. Sector-specific approaches and methodologies are essential to unlock their full potential.

16 To ensure MSUF2 has the potential to experience expected climate mitigation and adaptation benefits, it is important that the TAF will only invest in companies which in the company invests will meet the eligibility criteria established in the funding proposal (see Annex 8.3/11). Moreover, a rigorous approach as outlined in Annex 8.3/12), Mirova's approach integrates rigorous methodologies like Ex-Ac for carbon accounting and monitoring and evaluation frameworks to ensure alignment with climate goals.

The TAF is expected to enhance implementation of sustainable practices, maximizing on benefits through the use of sector-specific approaches and methodologies for MRV and monitoring and evaluation frameworks. including annual reviews and external audits have the potential to provide transparency and accountability throughout the investment life cycle. However, the question and answer procedure also demonstrate various weaknesses.

# Weaknesses in the MRV system

Overall, the MRV system described in annex II to the funding proposal is based on a limited set of state-of-the-art elements, such as the use of Tier 2 and Tier 3 data and remote-sensing technologies. This approach has several limitations that could undermine its effectiveness.

A key concern is reliance on self-reported data from portfolio companies, which may compromise the accuracy and reliability of the results. While cross-checking through site visits and external audits is included, these measures might not sufficiently cover the scale and geographical diversity of the projects.

Additionally, the methodology relies heavily on pre-defined mitigation metrics such as GHG removals. While mitigation metrics, such as GHG removals, are well-defined, adaptation indicators rely heavily on assumptions that may not be accurate or representative. This could potentially hinder the accurate evaluation of resilience-building impacts.

## Limited approach to tracing adaptation outcomes

The methodology in annex 2.3 for assessing adaptation outcomes (adaptation assessment) provides a structured approach, leveraging learnings from the LIDF while applying conservative assumptions and limited data. While the methodology demonstrates strengths and others where it could be improved for greater precision, there are several limitations that need to be addressed.

One challenge is the over-simplification of the diverse context and complexity of a broader and more varied MSJF portfolio. Another challenge is the heavy dependence on data provided by portfolio companies, which may not fully capture the full range of adaptation actions that impact the aggregated results (for example, the use of remote sensing data for carbon sequestration projects). Furthermore, while site visits can mitigate this challenge, but it remains unclear how regularly field visits will be conducted and whether they will cover all relevant sites. Additionally, the simplistic application of an average household size to estimate indirect beneficiaries overestimates the number of people potentially benefiting from adaptation actions.

In addition, the proposal is not clear about how the socioeconomic parameters related to adaptive capacity will be included in the quantification of beneficiaries. Collectively, these limitations highlight the need for a more comprehensive and robust set of metrics to strengthen the methodology's capacity to assess adaptation impacts comprehensively and ensure that it can effectively capture the full range of adaptation actions.

The proposal should consider incorporating additional data sources and metrics to strengthen the methodology's capacity to assess adaptation outcomes, taking into account the weaknesses highlighted above.


---
**Page 101**
---

## Integration of climate impact in investment decision-making

While all investments to be made by SIF/EF will be screened against climate eligibility, it remains somewhat unclear what priority the review of climate mitigation and adaptation potential will have in the screening process. The current GCF criteria for determining eligibility of investments against criteria set by GCF is a necessary but not sufficient step; in addition, there are no specific criteria to guide how investments with higher climate impact should be prioritized over those with lower climate impact. To address this issue, the SIF/EF will establish an Investment Committee and appoint one independent member with a background in climate adaptation finance to its Investment Committee to ensure outcome considerations have a strong voice and advocacy.

### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

#### Scale: Medium

The UN Decade of Sustainable Land Management has the potential to drive systemic change and contribute to the GCF paradigm shift by fostering sustainable land use practices across its target countries via a series of innovative investments in new and sustainable business models. It builds on the present trend towards more integrated approaches that combine climate-smart agriculture efforts to de-risk private-sector investors and leveraging TA to support the institutional and operational capacity building required for scaling up these approaches, such as agroforestry and regenerative agriculture, aiming to scale these practices through a combination of public-private partnerships. The programme is designed with a focus on land-based adaptation by aligning with the unique needs of high-risk hotspots.

The potential of the programme to effect paradigm shift however also faces inherent limitations:

- **Market barriers:** The funding proposal outlines a strategy to achieve systemic change by embedding sustainable land use practices into market and policy mechanisms. This is achieved through a combination of approaches, including de-risking investments, promoting certification standards, and aligning with national and international climate policies such as nationally determined contributions and Sustainable Development Goals. However, the programme does not currently plan for substantial efforts to influence broader policy reforms.
- **Institutional barriers:** For systemic change to extend beyond the project lifecycle, targeted actions are required to address institutional barriers that prevent or hinder unsustainable actions. This is a critical area of limitation for private sector-led programmes and needs to be addressed through the programme. The combination of market-based mechanisms and certification systems is promising but may face challenges in addressing structural market barriers.
- **Incentive barriers:** Effective change will require coordinated efforts with supply-chain actors, including buyers and processors, who are key stakeholders in the value chain for sustainable land use practices. The programme does not currently plan for substantial efforts on that front.

### 1.4 Enhancing smallholder and SMEs’ access to markets

The SIF/EF is exploring options to enhance smallholder and SMEs' (SMEs) market access through certification schemes (e.g., Forest Stewardship Council, Rainforest Alliance), supply-chain integration, and TA. These initiatives aim to improve the competitiveness of smallholders and SMEs in global and regional markets for commodities. However, certification schemes can be costly and complex for smallholders to implement. While the programme will provide technical assistance to help smallholder farmers adopt sustainable land management practices, ensuring that certified farmers are essential to prevent exclusion. It is unclear from the funding proposal what support specifically the programme will provide to smallholders and SMEs to defray related costs in.


---
**Page 102**
---

addition, the funding proposal identifies key buyers and supply-chain actors but does not elaborate on mechanisms to secure long-term take-off of agreements or enhance traceability, which are vital for sustained market access. Strengthening partnerships with buyers and integrating them into the programme would be a logical next step. The funding proposal implicitly assumes that certification will naturally attract buyers, but additional incentives (e.g., price premiums) may be needed to ensure sufficient demand. While the programme is not set up to provide such incentives but could make a sustained effort to build partnerships with key buyers.

Private investment dynamics and junior equity requirements: The programme seeks to reduce the perceived risk of investments in sustainable land use through concessional finance or guarantees, which would help attract private investors. However, the proposal reduced junior equity as the market becomes more familiar with these investments and their risk-return profile. While the formula on page 1026, the proposal does not provide concrete evidence that this assumption is valid. In fact, the AL suggests to include a large junior equity layer in MSFZ compared to the proposed 5% of total investment. This would help ensure sufficient demand for land reduction and financial returns from the predecessor fund; this assumption remains speculative.

To lower the risk profile further, the programme could consider other instruments such as reduced default rates, stable returns, and market validation of sustainable practices. Also, the programme should consider diversifying its portfolio by investing in a wider range of local or regional investment sources in target countries. Diversifying the investor base and building local financial capacity could reduce future dependency on high junior equity thresholds.

In conclusion, the MSFZ proposal effectively demonstrates paradigm shift potential by addressing key challenges for scaling up sustainable land management (SLM) with its approach to scalability, reliability, and long-term sustainability. The fund is well-positioned to catalyse transformative change in target countries. Overall, the TAP rates the potential for the project to effect a paradigm shift as medium.

### Sustainable development potential

The MSFZ presents a strong case for delivering sustainable development benefits across economic, social, and environmental dimensions.

#### Environmental co-benefits: The fund aims to deliver significant environmental benefits, including biodiversity conservation and improved land productivity. This translates into broader climate resilience and ecosystem services, which will be achieved via legally binding covenants in MSFZ investments that preserve minimum targets of land to be preserved and protected. These environmental co-benefits are well-articulated; their synergies with social and economic objectives could be better integrated. For example, the fund's focus on improving local livelihoods could enhance co-benefits.

#### Social co-benefits: The emphasis of MSFZ on social co-benefits, including long-term quality of life through improved labour conditions and sustainable livelihoods, reflects a commendable commitment to enhancing social outcomes in the target countries. The fund's focus on improving living wage assessments for Rainforest Alliance-certified investors demonstrates alignment with international best practices. However, there are challenges related to understanding contextual challenges in the target countries and sectors. Seasonal and daily workers, who are among the most vulnerable, may face significant restrictions due to lack of compliance across diverse regulatory landscapes where enforcement is often weak. Additionally, efforts to close living wage gaps within three years are ambitious but may be hindered by structural barriers


---
**Page 103**
---

26. Gender-sensitive development impact: The programme demonstrates a robust commitment to gender equality, with at least 30 per cent of its portfolio meeting the 2X Challenge criteria by the end of the fund's lifecycle. This alignment will be facilitated through pre-screening and monitoring of project proposals against the 2X Challenge framework, and TA. A strength of the programme lies in its structured action plan, which includes specific indicators for gender equality and women’s empowerment, as well as a dedicated Gender Action Plan and report on sex-disaggregated data, ensuring transparency and accountability.

The fund critically assesses projects based on their alignment with the 2X Challenge criteria against recognized global best practices. However, the target of 50 per cent portfolio alignment lacks a defined timeline or milestones to track progress effectively. The proposal does not clearly outline strategies to address gender equality in all aspects of project design and implementation.

1.3 Key economic benefits: The programme highlights potential job creation and income generation as key economic co-benefits. It estimates substantial employment opportunities through project activities and increased income for smallholder farmers. More specifically, MEF 2.0 projects are expected to create approximately 547,869 jobs directly or indirectly. These calculations are based on historical data from 13 LMFIP investments, with conservative estimates of job creation in the current portfolio. While the proposal outlines ambitious aggregate targets, it does not detail specific mechanisms to track income improvements or set baseline levels for comparison.

In addition to employment and income generation, the programme also provides information on how the fund will secure market access for smallholders beyond the project lifecycle.

16. The TIAF rates the sustainable development impact potential of the project as medium-high.
### Needs of the recipient
Scale: Medium-High

29. The proposal targets vulnerabilities and financing gaps in the AFU/SFU sectors across target countries. While diverse, all countries face relevant challenges, including land degradation, water scarcity, and climate change. For example, Ethiopia reports that 86 per cent of its national emissions are attributed to AFU/SFU-related activities. The fund's emphasis on addressing these issues is consistent with the SLM and agroforestry practices demonstrated in countries building climate resilience and adaptation.

30. While the 11 countries included in this programme all face challenges related to climate change, there are significant differences between them regarding their development status and climate-related needs. Target countries include lower-middle-income to upper-middle-income economies, with substantial variations in their gross domestic product per capita and their share of national emissions from AFU/SFU activities. This diversity highlights the differing capacities and requirements for climate action across the region. ### Country ownership

The AIF has submitted no-objection letters (NOLs) from national designated authorities in 7 of the 11 target countries: Costa Rica, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Malawi, Morocco, Peru and the Philippines. The GCF is currently in active discussions with other countries regarding NOLs but there is no certainty that additional NOLs will be submitted.

MSLF2 otherwise demonstrates country ownership through its design, which is tailored to address the specific needs and priorities of its target countries. These needs have been identified by engaging with national designated authorities, line ministries, civil society organizations, and private-sector actors. The GCF has also supported the development of national strategies for land use change mitigation and climate-smart agriculture, as well as country-specific adaptation strategies of its participating countries, ensuring support for their commitments to sustainable land use and climate action.

During implementation, by engaging with public authorities and local institutions, MSLF2 aims to build capacity in critical areas such as SLM, monitoring and reporting of land-use change mitigation activities, and the development of national strategies. This will be supported through regular dialogue by portfolio companies with stakeholders, supported by a detailed stakeholder engagement plan. The GCF has also provided technical assistance for the development of national strategies for Indigenous and affected communities. This will be supported via the TAF which is predominantly geared at providing post-implementation support and capacity-building to portfolio companies in these areas.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

The lack of a clearly defined aperture of portfolio companies to be supported by MSLF2 limits the ability to assess the programme's economic soundness with certainty. Nevertheless, the following observations can be made:

- **AIF equity commitment provisions:** With a EUR 10 million equity commitment from GCF (split across both a junior and senior equity component) and a total expected fund size of EUR 50 million, MSLF2 represents a relatively small allocation of 1% to the AIF portfolio which is in line with similar blended finance structures on the market. However, given that GCF proceeds as well as the capital's first loss function will be ringfenced (likely in the form of a sub-fund under the AIF), the maximum allowable share of GCF resources can go up to 40% of the fund size. The GCF has committed EUR 1 million to MSLF2, which represents less than half of the entire GCF equity commitment to the sub-fund). ITAP notes however that the maximum share of


---
**Page 106**
---

junior capital to the GCF will be capped at 25% (for the entire fund, and the sub-fund). It is important to point

Distribution waterfall. The distribution waterfall of the draft term sheet prioritizes senior equity investors for both principal recovery and preferred returns before junior equity investors receive any return on their investment. This structure may not be appropriate in all GCF models seen in other GCF-supported funds, which typically split principal and return between senior and junior equity investors. If this structure remains unchanged, a 25% cap on senior equity layer may create inefficiencies by incentivizing the fund to invest in projects with lower returns than would otherwise be possible.

The GCF Secretariat should negotiate for a more balanced waterfall or, failing that, refrain from agreeing to a 25% cap on senior equity layer.

Climate benefits and cost efficiency. The projected climate benefits include direct GHG removals of 17 million tCO₂ eq over the project lifetime and significant social benefits including improved health outcomes and increased access to clean water. The estimated costs are €0.42 per ton of CO₂ removed based on the GCF commitment, and EUR 20.11 per ton based on the loan cost. The cost of removing one ton of CO₂ is significantly lower than the cost of alternative investments in AFOLU sectors varies widely based on geographic, project-specific, and market conditions. A cost range of USD 10 to USD 30 per ton of CO₂ eq is considered reasonable; thus, the project is expected to be competitive.

Overall, while uncertainties around the pipeline and some structural features of the fund prevent a definitive assessment of its cost efficiency, the projected climate benefits are high and this compares favourably with market benchmarks. Strategic adjustments to the distribution waterfall and junior equity provisions would enhance the funds’ efficiency and attractiveness. The ITAP therefore considers the efficiency and effectiveness of the programme as medium.

### Overall remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

Overall, the funding proposal is comprehensive, and although there are various risks and uncertainties (as highlighted in the previous section), these do not appear to be material outcomes.

To address some of the identified weaknesses, the ITAP recommends that the GCF Secretariat should advocate for a more balanced distribution waterfall structure for MSLF2, ensuring a fair allocation of principal recovery and preferred returns between senior and junior equity investors. If this structural adjustment cannot be achieved, the Secretariat should reject the proposed 25% cap on senior equity threshold, as it risks incentivizing the fund to invest in projects with lower returns than would otherwise be possible.

Furthermore, the ITAP recommends to the Board inclusion of the following conditions precedent to first disbursement of GCF proceeds in the funded activity agreement:

(a) Delivery to the GCF by the Accredited Entity, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of all documentation required for project approval under MSLF2 which shall include: (i) a comprehensive framework outlining project-specific metrics for carbon removal; (ii) further details on the fallback methodology based on Ex- AET 1.0, as described in Board decision B33/12; (iii) further details on the fallback methodology based on Ex-AET 1.0, as described in Board decision B33/12; and (iv) a framework that will be applied in cases where specific data from portfolio companies is unavailable; and (v) commitment to continue monitoring the reliability of carbon removal estimates under varying data quality and project contexts, with explanations of how these analyses address uncertainties;


---
**Page 107**
---

(b) Deliver to the GCF by the Accredited Entity, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of a detailed plan and/or system for tracking and accounting for adaptation outcomes in the MSLE2 portfolio which shall include: (i) a framework outlining the methodology for tracking and accounting for adaptation outcomes; (ii) adjustments to account for regional variations, socioeconomic parameters, and adaptive capacity specificities; (iii) a proposed frequency of field visits to ensure data collection is timely and consistent, including a proposed frequency and scope of field visits, the aspects to be verified, and the roles and responsibilities of personnel conducting these visits.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP263)

## Proposal name:
Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2(MSLF2)

## Accredited entity:
Mirova

## Country/(s):
Costa Rica, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Malaysia, Morocco, Peru, Philippines.

## Project/Programme size:
EUR 36.3 million

### Impact potential
Mirova acknowledges that the approach presented in the funding proposal still presents room for improvement to ensure MSLF2 has the potential to generate expected climate mitigation and adaptation outcomes. In that regard, ITAP’s assessment clearly helps refining MSLF2 approach and provides a clear direction on what focus on before launching the Programme.

The following improvement points are well taken:

- **Carbon Removal Calculation Methodology:** The absence of a customized model for carbon removal calculation may lead to inaccurate estimates and compliance with GCF standards. To address these issues, it is indeed recommended that Mirova develops its own model and incorporate sensitivity analyses to manage uncertainties. Mirova is already engaging with the relevant stakeholders and partners to ensure Mirova is also working to refine any assumptions related to the projections.

- **Monitoring, Reporting, and Verification (MRV) System:** The current MRV system is considered robust but may still lead to some degree of compromise accuracy. To enhance the system’s robustness, it is recommended implementing a more comprehensive approach for reporting and tracking the adoption of adaptation outcomes by developing clearer, quantitative indicators. The MRV system will be reviewed and upgraded in line with the approach of the Programme and GCF standards. Mirova is also considering reviewing the MRV systems employed by projects within our carbon funds as part of our investigation into options to address this issue.

- **Assessment of Adaptation Outcomes:** The methodology for evaluating adaptation impacts needs refinement, as it currently relies too much on generalization rather than specific criteria. To ensure a more targeted approach to measuring adaptation outcomes is necessary, including clearer criteria and indicators that reflect the different elements of climate change factors related to adaptation. Mirova is in the process of refining our approach, including developing a more comprehensive framework which adequately reflects the different elements of climate change adaptation while also being applicable across various project contexts. Further details will be provided in the upgraded MRV system including on income generation aspects which is being piloted on some LBNF projects.

### Paradigm shift potential


---
**Page 109**
---

The ITAP’s medium rating of the Programme’s paradigm shifting potential is noted.

As mentioned in the commentary, MSLF2 is specifically focused on enhancing the competitiveness and sustainability of smallholder agriculture to increase its contribution to the global supply chain. We recognize the ITAP's observation regarding the necessity to integrate environmental co-benefits into the project design, more broadly, to secure long-term off-taker agreements and bolster partnerships with buyers. Since a strong relationship with local governments has been established, as Mirova is already active in all the countries involved, we are confident that this will be an important element of our future investment connections.

In response to ITAP’s comments on private capital mobilization, we are fully aligned with the GCF's objective of minimizing the junior capital requirement as much as possible. Fundraising is progressing well, and we are confident in our ability to attract large-scale investors to meet our target. We have already secured commitments from a number of institutional investors using experience from our predecessor fund, the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund, as well as the knowledge gained through our work with the GCF. The level of junior capital mobilization is similar by similar funds in the market. Additionally, we have compared the MSLF2 Fund’s waterfall structure to other funds and found that it is more attractive for institutional investors due to its innovative structures. Mirova's Research Center has formed a research partnership with Columbia University to further explore the potential of blended finance. We will publish our paper on natural capital blended finance soon.

The level of junior protection integrates also private investors feedback: i) the sector is perceived as highly innovative and risky; ii) risks presented by the project are not fully mitigated at the time of investment, as was the case in the past. We agree with ITAP’s observation that the success of MSLF2, particularly environmental co-benefits, will be dependent on the ability to attract institutional investors.

It is also worth noting that Mirova has a global investor base, not limited to Europe; we are currently in discussions with several European and non-European institutions. We have observed any significant differences in their junior protection requirements compared to those of European investors.


### Sustainable development potential

Mirova welcomes the positive feedback on the environmental, social, gender and economic co-benefits of the MSLF2 Programme as well as the identified challenges which are very relevant and would be useful suggestions to enhance MSLF2 approach on:

- **Environmental co-benefit:** The idea of integrating biodiversity conservation and improvement into the project design is highly appreciated. This will be further articulated to create further synergies and will be considered for those projects estimated in the future.
  
- **Social co-benefit:** Mirova fully agrees on the challenges mentioned which were also identified with the LDFE: MSLF2 will build on that experience to engage further with the social dimension of the project. # Needs of the recipient

Mirova acknowledges ITAP’s favorable evaluation and observations regarding the recipient’s needs, which have been rated as “medium-high” by ITAP. Notably, ITAP emphasizes the diverse array of potential projects that could be developed in the future to address both the challenges and opportunities. A crucial factor for the success of MSLF2, as highlighted by ITAP is the diversification of the portfolio beyond land degradation neutrality (LDN) projects. This not only serves as a risk mitigator for private investors but also enhances the overall impact of the fund. To facilitate this diversification, legal documentation includes concentration limits by geography.

## Country ownership

The ITAP’s medium-high rating of the Programme’s Country ownership is noted. Concerning the two areas highlighted in the ITAP’s assessment (stakeholder engagement and knowledge sharing), we can consider incorporating these areas into the scope of the Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) for MSLF2. The TAF will be part of the ESD’s portfolio, and strengthening local capacity is typically included in the ESD as an implementation tool with investors.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

The ITAP’s medium rating of the Programme’s Efficiency and Effectiveness is noted. The parameters for the proposed finance structure for MSLF2 were developed through both a top-down and a bottom-up approach: (i) the top-down approach involved identifying best practices and comparing them to the existing framework, leading to the identification of “high risk” tranches; and above all (ii) the bottom-up approach incorporated feedback from both public and private investors. The resulting structure aims to facilitate the mobilization of private capital without providing excessive protection with the proposed parameters for MSLF2 (over 50% of total capital as maximum). These parameters are intended to encourage private investors, including those from the initial vintage, to invest in MSLF2, while ensuring that the fund is not viewed as risky. These reflect the outcomes of negotiation and consensus among investors, and we can confirm the commitment of both existing investors from the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund (LDN) and new investors from the private sector.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

We greatly appreciate the ITAP’s feedback and look forward to the opportunity for continued partnership with GCF. Regarding the recommendations on distribution structure and preference for a top-down approach, we believe that this is an appropriate way of proceeding in order to ensure the mobilization of junior capital requirement as much as possible (25% of total capital is maximum). We believe that the proposed parameters are reasonable given the need to attract private concessionary capital without being able to attract private capital. Mirova has a significant competitive advantage over other funds in terms of its ability to mobilize and leverage resources from private institutional investors. Mirova has the ability to mobilize private capital from mainstream investors, as demonstrated by the Land Degradation Neutrality Fund, which had over 50% of its total capital invested by private investors at the time of the closing ceremony that took place in July 2024. Mirova has already mobilized mainstream private institutional


---
**Page 111**
---

investors, including large European insurance companies, confirming the willingness of private investors to commit to the proposed level of junior capital. Market feedback and interviews with large private institutional investors clearly indicate their requirements regarding the leverage ratio and the need for a more detailed calculation methodology.

Regarding the carbon removal calculation methodology, we are focused on enhancing it by developing a comprehensive framework and providing more detailed calculations. This will also include scenario-based analyses to illustrate the reliability of carbon removal estimates across different scenarios.

In addition, we are developing a comprehensive plan or system for tracking and accounting for adaptation outcomes within the MS&2 portfolios, which will encompass: (1) a framework that outlines methodologies for assessing adaptation; (2) a set of indicators for monitoring and adjusting for regional differences, socioeconomic factors, and adaptive capacity unique to each country, along with clear guidelines.
