# Introduction

This document provides an assessment of the barriers and opportunities for advancing gender inclusion in the Micro Sustainable Land Fund 2 programme (MSLF 2). The assessment includes an overview of the key gender-related barriers in the context of land degradation, deforestation and sustainable agriculture. The assessment also discusses the opportunities for adopting a gender-sensitive approach to address these barriers and promote women's equal access inclusiveness in the forestry and sustainable agriculture sectors and alignment with industry standards. This document outlines key entry points for the MSLF 2, followed by in-country assessments and an endline target analysis.

The country assessment provides an overview of the gender equality status at each country level, including employment opportunities, women's participation in agribusiness and forestry sectors, and on gender issues on land rights.


## Methodology

The gender assessment has been prepared based on the guiding questions listed in the GCF gender policy documents and templates (instantly the Mainstreaming Toolkit) and on the Updated GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment is based on quantitative data and qualitative evidence derived from a desk review of existing literature and secondary resources.


### 1.1 Gender and Land Degradation

Land degradation is one of the most pressing environmental problems across the world and it affects the livelihoods of billions of people, particularly women, smallholder farmers, people living in rural communities and the poorest and most marginalised populations. Land degradation has a significant impact on food security, water resources and biodiversity loss. Women are twice more affected by drought, land degradation and deforestation. The literature on this topic in fact illustrates a strong link between gender equality and land rights, access to productive assets, women's rights to resources and incentives, opportunities to participate in decision-making, and the distribution of costs/benefits of projects targeting land improvement.

Many poor people, especially women, girls, and indigenous peoples, depend heavily on the natural resources that are threatened by land degradation and these are worsened by climate change. Almost 80% of employed women in least-developed countries rely on agriculture as their main source of income.


---
**Page 116**
---

their primary livelihood and women represent the majority of people who rely on land in regions affected by deforestation, land degradation and droughts.*

The impact of deforestation affects men and women in different ways, and evidence across Africa and Asia show that women are more likely to be impacted by desertification. For example, deforestation and desertification increase the amount of time that women spend gathering fuelwood and fetching water. Desertification is also leading to loss of efficiency in tasks traditionally performed outdoors (e.g., cooking, cleaning) as well as a decline in the availability of firewood sources and farming activities (linked to increased labour needed to combat desertification). Some of the gender impacts of land degradation and droughts include an increase in women’s workload, reduced access to water, reduced quantity of food, increased exclusion from decision-making spaces and school drop-out rates.

Across all countries, histories of land tenure conflicts, ineffective land governance systems, and patriarchal norms that discriminate against women and girls prevent women from benefiting from land reforms. In many African countries, women are excluded from owning or controlling land. In some countries, recent studies find that across the world women continue to face discrimination in their right to own, use and control land.* In East Asia, women continue facing obstacles to accessing land and non-farm assets. Women’s rights to land vary widely by region, reflecting differences around land ownership, and legal discrimination under customary law. All sub-Saharan African countries show evidence of some form of discrimination, notably under customary law, which prevents women from owning or controlling land in their own name. In many cases, women inherit or own land under their name. When women are widowed, they often suffer evictions from in-laws and are left with no land from which they can generate income and sustain their families. Women’s rights to land vary widely by region, reflecting differences around land ownership.

Women’s rights to land vary widely by region, reflecting differences around land ownership. In 100 countries across the world. Many Latin American countries have considered advanced legal frameworks to ensure equal access to land rights.* Most legal frameworks in the region entitle women and men equally to land rights. Women in some African countries have also gained rights but have benefited from strengthened equality provisions in laws addressing women’s status within the family and within marriage. Yet, even in countries where women have the same legal rights to own and access land only a smaller percentage of farm ownership falls in the hands of women.

* Nanashiro-Minami, E. 2021 Gender and Land Restoration. UNCCD Global Land Outlook Working Paper. https://www.unccd.int/sites/default/files/2022-03/UNCCD-GLO-09-Nanashiro.pdf

* FAO (2017) Gender and sustainable development in drylands: an analysis of field experiences. Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations.

* UNCCD (2022) Study on the differentiated impacts of land degradation, desertification and drought on women and men. Retrieved at https://www.unccd.int/sites/default/files/2022-10/Study%20on%20Gender%20and%20

* Ibid.

5


---
**Page 117**
---

Research conducted by the World Bank found that even when women own or manage land, gender-related differences remain in agricultural productivity, with male farmers producing more than women farmers across several African countries. This is linked to gender gaps in access to new agricultural technology or credit and investments for sustainable land management such as soil conservation and augmentation, terracing, tree planting and establishment of agroforestry systems.

Without an appropriate integration of gender considerations in land restoration and sustainable land use initiatives there is not just a risk of limiting impact but of exacerbating inequalities. For example, where women do not legally own their land, or where customs and practices limit their access to resources, they are often excluded from decision-making as equal actors. As a result, they are excluded from access to extension services and inputs. Women’s limited or insecure land rights undermine their engagement in reforestation, forestry and agroforestry systems, which has direct costs for their food security and nutrition, and that of their families.

Evidence compiled by the United Nations Convention to Combat Deforestation (UNCCD) indicates that degradation threats can be reduced if gender gaps in land rights, access to finance and credit, particularly microfinance services, and information and knowledge dissemination, were closed. In fact, equitable land governance, land tenure and land tenure security are all fundamental components to enable land restoration efforts.

### 1.2 Gender, Deforestation and Forest Degradation

Women and men use and manage forests in different ways, with socioeconomic and sociocultural environments playing a crucial role. The use of and control over resources, as well as the prevailing power structures, decision-making processes, and subsistence strategies are also affected by gender differences. People living near forested areas often have limited access to land and other natural resources, which can be attributed in part to gender inequalities. This is particularly true for women who may rely for up to half of their income on forests, and thus require secure access and use rights to these resources. In addition, socio-cultural norms often make it difficult for women to participate equally with men in forest-related activities or decision-making processes.

Across different countries, often men control the most valuable forest resources such as timber production and other products that can be sold to the market, while women’s control is usually


---
**Page 118**
---

Gender Assessment for the Mireva Sustainable Land Fund 2

centered on fuelwood, fodder and non-timber products.[1][2] Deforestation and forest degradation have a direct impact on women's lives because it often means that women have to walk longer distances to collect fuelwood, negatively impacting their income-generation opportunities, and personal safety.

Several barriers hinder women's participation in sustainable forestry management and reforestation efforts. Discriminatory land ownership laws and tenancy agreements represent a challenge for women who are unable to own or control land and trees and subsequently resulting in unequal benefits from forest restoration, plantation and agro-forestry investments. The lack of land ownership titles and formal tenure reduce women's decision-making power over the use and management of forests.

Men and women also have different household responsibilities and gendered division of labour has a direct effect on their preferences for planting trees and for their engagement in value-chain activities. For example, studies have shown that men prefer planting trees that offer higher returns or are more marketable than those preferred by women who focus more on their use and relevance for subsistence of the family such as trees that provide fruits, firewood, fodder and increase soil fertility. Women's time poverty and physical safety concerns limit their access to forest resources and they have less control over the proceeds from tree products which limits the time women can allocate to forestry and agroforestry income-generating activities.

Another difference between how men and women participate in forest value chains is related to the size and types of businesses they engage with. Women's participation in forest value chain opportunities tends to be more informal than that of men who are more likely to own and manage larger businesses.[3] This varies across regions. In Africa, for example, women dominate the collection of forest products, but in Latin America men are predominantly engaged in commercial activities such as logging and timber production. Women's engagement tends to decrease as the scale of operations increases. Women's participation also diminishes when the distance to the trading location increases, often due to socio-cultural norms.

Given these differences between how men and women engage in forest value chains, ensuring women's participation in sustainable land management is crucial for promoting sustainability and management, enhancing biodiversity, and improving rural livelihoods.


---
**Page 119**
---

# Gender Assessment for the Mireva Sustainable Land Fund 2

## 1.3 Gender and Sustainable Agriculture

Women play important roles in global, regional and local supply chains, in farms and processing plants. Although women contribute significantly to these sectors, they often remain invisible, and women are overly represented in lower paid workforces and smallholder farming. As key actors in the rural food production chain across many countries and value chains, women’s contributions to sustainable land management (SLM) are critical. For small farmers that rely on agriculture and natural resources for their livelihoods, the gender-differentiated impacts of climate change can intensify the disadvantages they already have.

Women contend with additional barriers such as discriminatory cultural practices and social norms, which limit their access to productive resources and decision-making power. Gender gaps are also significant in access to productive resources such as land, inputs, markets, technology, education, and financial services. Although women make up around 43% of the agricultural labor force globally, they own less than 20% of the land in developing countries[1], but women only represent 30% of cooperative memberships and just 15% of landholders globally.[2] As a result of these barriers women adopt sustainable land management (SLM) practices at lower rates compared to men. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making about SLM, especially in Sub-Saharan Africa[3]. Women’s contributions to farming, such as household labour, childcare, and post-harvest processing, are often unpaid or undervalued. This reinforces roles and contributes to the gender pay gap within agriculture. Women are also less likely than men to plant high-value crops, which may result from limited access to climate change adaptation tools and extension services[4]. Even when they are employed in the agricultural sector, women are faced with gender-based discrimination and gender-based violence and harassment at work.[5] Women face significant challenges related to sexual harassment, exploitation, harassment, and unequal treatment in terms of employment opportunities, wages, and leadership roles within cooperatives, businesses, and industry associations.

[3] Seagull (2022). Women at more risk of effects of deforestation, land degradation, drought and climate change. Available at https://www.seagull.com.au/news/women-at-more-risk-of-effects-of-deforestation-land-degradation-drought-new-un-co study

[4] The World Bank. Gender gap in agricultural productivity in Sub-Saharan Africa: causes, costs and solutions. Available at https://www.unctad.org/sites/default/files/technical%20papers/Decisions&Libra Publication 19316.pdf

# 1.4 Addressing gender gaps to achieve sustainable land management

To address the several gender barriers mentioned in the previous sections, entry points exist for integrating gender issues into land degradation efforts and in sustainable land use projects overall. These include (list non-exhaustive):

- Understanding the differing roles, responsibilities, and knowledge in managing natural resources between men and women
- Addressing gender differences in rights and access to land and other natural resources
- Facilitating women's access to land for tree planting and agroforestry activities by advocating for their inclusion in land tenure systems, providing technical assistance or facilitating community-based land management approaches that prioritise women’s inclusion
- Addressing gendered access to, and interventions regarding, new technology, information and training related to natural resource management; by providing equal access to inputs, technologies, and land, women can enhance their productivity, increase their income, and improve food security
- Addressing forms of cooperation, conflict or controversy between men and women or different ethnic groups arising from land degradation
- Involving women in the design and decision-making processes at all levels; and strengthening gender equality through these processes
- Providing women with access to financial resources to overcome financial barriers to participating in reforestation and agroforestry projects. Financial support can be used for purchasing tree seedlings, investing in agroforestry inputs, or implementing sustainable land management practices
- Integrating gender into extension services tailored to women’s needs that can enhance their capacity to engage in reforestation and agroforestry activities effectively. This includes training on tree species selection, planting techniques, post-establishment care, and the processing of agroforestry products. Close knowledge gaps and enhance skills development of women for agricultural technologies, climate-smart agricultural practices, financial literacy, and business management
- Promoting women’s participation in decision-making processes related to reforestation and agroforestry planning, implementation, and monitoring is crucial. This includes their inclusion as members of local forest committees, community meetings, and project management teams

These opportunities listed above represent some of the necessary actions to close gender gaps, promote women's empowerment and so successfully implement sustainable land management and regenerative agriculture practices. Mirova recognises that an in-depth

# Gender Assessment for the Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2

understanding of the gender barriers, gaps and opportunities in each specific context will be needed to inform gender responsive investments. The following sections illustrate how Mirova's approach in the Sustainable Land Fund 2 will ensure that projects address the specific gender barriers present in their contexts of implementation, and that they integrate gender considerations across their business operations.

## Programme Overview

### 2.1 Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2

The Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2 (MSLF2), a private sector-led, multi-country investment fund that supports private sector operators in developing nations to accelerate climate action in the agriculture and land use sectors. MSLF2's investments contribute to the achievement of Paris Agreement objectives by addressing the drivers of deforestation and land degradation, supporting sustainable land management practices, and promoting the conservation and restoration of ecosystem services in the context of climate hazards. MSLF2 will support agroforestry, sustainable forestry, and regenerative agriculture projects in developing countries, while preserving and restoring natural habitats.

The MSLF2 programme is structured around two main components. Component 1 focuses on the establishment and operationalisation of the investment vehicle, including disbursement of capital to a selected pipeline of investees. The investment vehicle will secure co-financing and invest directly in emerging economy businesses that adopt sustainable land management and regenerative agriculture practices. Activities under Component 1 include the development of the investment vehicle, as well as post-investment technical assistance. Component 2 focuses on the provision of technical assistance for investment readiness and impact enhancement support to portfolio companies after investment. Activities under Component 2 will include the identification of potential investees, as well as pre- and post-investment technical assistance. These activities will leverage the resources and capabilities of existing delivery partners and will also include the establishment and operationalisation of a Technical Assistance Facility.

### 2.2 Mirova ESG and Gender Policy

The MSLF2 applies Mirova's ESG Policy which guides the deployment of the Fund. It is complemented by Mirova’s wider minimum standards in its capacity as both holding company of Mirova and AIFM (Alternative Investment Fund Managers) of the MSLF2 Fund. The ESG Policy aligns with Mirova’s broader ESG principles, and MSLF2's ESG Team is responsible for working with the organisation. While Mirova has assigned the oversight responsibility for this ESG Policy to its Management Team, the ESG & Impact Manager ensures its operational implementation and updates.


---
**Page 122**
---

Gender Assessment for the Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2

The Policy requires investors companies to comply with national and international standards (e.g., the International Labour Organization’s Core Conventions on child labour, forced labour, equality and rights of association; the IFC Social and Environmental Performance Standards, the IFC’s EHS General Guidelines and the relevant sector specific IFC EHS Guidelines). Mirova implements its ESG policies in line with the United Nations Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights.

## Learnings from Mirova's Land Degradation Neutrality Fund

The approach for gender consideration in the MSF2 builds on the experience and lessons learned from Mirova’s Land Degradation Neutrality Fund (LDN). The LDN Fund’s approach to gender is based on the UNCCD Gender Strategy, which outlines four priority areas to incorporate in LDN implementation[1]. The gender approach also considers the UNCCD manual that provides guidance for mainstreaming gender in LDN projects. In line with the high ambition for gender integration into the fund, in 2021, Mirova and IDH, the manager of the LDN Technical Assistance Facility issued a Request for Proposals targeted at women-led enterprises.

Further, in September 2022, Mirova became a member of 2X Global with the objective of leveraging this unique global network to engage in peer learning and knowledge exchange on practical insights and good practice, and benefit from co-investing and co-financing opportunities. The LDN Fund was one of the first funds to be certified as meeting the 2X criteria for gender equality. In 2023, 71% of the beneficiaries reached by the fund met the leadership criterion. Of women beneficiaries reached in 2023, 44% of the people benefiting from the investments were women (excluding employees), while in 2021 54% of beneficiaries were women. This increase is largely attributed to the addition of one investor that outperformed the overall portfolio in terms of gender inclusion of women in their supply chain.

Based on the learnings from the previous fund, the MSF2 will maintain a similar approach and high commitment to gender equality by tracking the gender responsiveness of each investment and the achievement of 2X criteria.

### Key entry points for a gender responsive MSF 2

The integration of a gender perspective in MSF 2 follows best practices and alignment with industry standards for land degradation neutrality projects and programmes. The following entry points are relevant for MSF 2:

- Reach women beneficiaries and involve women at all levels of the workforce
- and in value chains through the development of investment-level gender

## 3. Gender approach for the programme

### 3.1 Programmatic approach

The MSUF2 builds on Mirova's philosophy and dedication to responsible investing, based on the notion of impact and indirectly encouraging and fostering better environmental, social and governance (ESG) practices is essential to creating long-term value for society as a whole.

The fundamentals of the MSUF2 strategy are to invest in sustainable land-use projects and companies in the forestry and agriculture sector, while targeting positive impacts in terms of climate mitigation and adaptation as well as co-benefits in biodiversity protection, community inclusion and gender equality.

Mirova faces ESG risks - financial, legal and/or reputational - directly as a company and indirectly as a Fund Manager due to the actions of its investees. In response, Mirova acknowledges that it has an important role to play in promoting and supporting the implementation of robust ESG management. All ESG risks, whether stemming from Mirova's own practices or through its investees, will be handled in accordance with this ESG Policy.

The MSUF2 ESG Policy provides guidance for the implementation of MSUF2 and applies to Mirova as well as all investee companies. The MSUF2 Fund Managers will commit to ongoing improvements in their environmental, social, and governance aspects.


---
**Page 124**
---

# Gender Assessment for the Mirova Sustainable Land Fund 2

They will work diligently to address any gaps identified during due diligence in comparison to relevant international best practice standards, setting appropriate targets and timelines for achieving them.

## Alignment with GCF Gender Policy

The Fund aligns with GCF Gender Policy[1] which aims to address and reduce gender inequalities, deepen stakeholder engagement and deliver better accountability to both men and women to generate sustainable livelihood opportunities, health and well-being, and resilience in the face of climate change risks. Mirova is responsible for supporting the implementation of the Fund-level gender action plan and for reporting on results, including the generation and use of sex-disaggregated data.

In line with GCF project-level requirements, Miriva will:

- Prepare a gender assessment and gender action plan for the Fund (this Annex) and integrate an analysis of context and sociocultural factors underlying climate change-exacerbated gender inequalities.
- During the Fund's implementation, Miriva will refine as necessary the gender-related baseline, indicators and targets.
- Miriva will monitor progress and report on the progress made in implementing the project level gender action plan.

Miriva will also ensure that each project funded under MSLF2 is aligned with national policies and priorities on gender and that these are reflected in the contextual analysis conducted during the screening phase.

## Alignment and contribution to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

The MSLF2 will contribute to SDGS – to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls. The Miriva Fund 2 will aim at contributing to the achievement of Target 5.5: "Ensure women's full and effective participation and equal opportunities for leadership at all levels of decision-making in political, economic and public life" by tracking the proportion of women in management positions.

The project will also indirectly contribute to end forms of discrimination against women through the support provided to investee companies and their application of inclusive

**Page 125**

3.2 MSLF2 Gender Lens Investing Thesis

The MSLF2 approach ensures that activities are established on a foundation of identifying, minimizing and mitigating social and gender risks, underpinned by sound and accountable governance. In alignment with the ESG policy, the gender approach requires investments in the MSLF2 to:

- Comply with national environmental, social and labour laws and regulations.
- Comply with the Fund-level ESG Policy (which includes any ESG requirements of investors in the fund) and all issue-specific Policies.
- Meet the criteria of internationally recognized sustainability standards such as the IFC Social & Environmental Performance Standards, including GBV and SEAH prevention measures and policies that prevent, address, or eliminate SEAH-related risks.

Eligible investments must be land-based projects that fulfill and demonstrate several eligibility criteria, including:

- Demonstrated benefits: projects should clearly benefit local communities (job creation, income generation).
- Environmental & Social (ESG) risk management: robust ESG Standards are an integral part of the Fund's approach, and ensure the responsible handling of ESG risk.

To meet these requirements, MSLF2 applies a gender and social inclusion lens at different stages of the investment process and focuses on supporting investee companies to meet their gender and social inclusion commitments in line with the Fund’s policy.

At the Investment process level: ESG considerations are integrated from screening and due diligence phase, and gender-related milestones are integrated into contracting. MSLF2 fund is therefore committed to apply a gender and social inclusion lens in its investment process and foster its implementation through the following steps:

- Conduct a contextual analysis at the country and sector level using publicly available information prior to the screening phase.


---
**Page 126**
---

Gender Assessment for the Mireva Sustainable Land Fund 2

- Assess gender risks and impacts at project level during the due diligence phase, including external SHAW-related risks.
- Include a requirement on Gender Action Plan and women's empowerment in the contractual Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP)

At the project level, a Gender Action Plan is seen as a key item to:
- Operationalize the constraints and opportunities for women and men identified during due diligence
- Serve as a project manual to ensure women's equal participation, management in and enjoyment of project activities and benefits, with clear responsibilities and timelines for implementation
- Require periodic reviews of gender action implementation

At the portfolio level, alignment with international industry standards will be tracked, striving for 2X Challenge compliance for the majority of the portfolio. Finally, there will be opportunities to share lessons learned from the initiative in terms of what strategies are working well, what is falling behind, and what successful factors can be captured in the implementation of the gender action plans.

Alignment with 2X Challenge Criteria

The 2X Challenge was launched at the G7 Summit 2018 as a bold commitment to inspire DFIs and the broader private sector to invest with a gender lens. To date, the initiative has raised gender lens investments by over $5 billion in Africa alone, bringing public and private sector investors together to unlock gender-smart capital at scale.

The 2X Challenge Criteria are now a globally recognised minimum standard for gender inclusion, defining what gender inclusion looks like across industries. It specifically focuses on evaluating gender inclusion across five areas: Entrepreneurship and ownership; leadership; employment; supply chain and products/services.

#### Products & Services
- **A12. Product or service explicitly aimed at benefitting women/girls and/or driving gender equity**: Yes/No

## Figure 3: ZXCriteria, updated as of February 2024

The ZX Criteria is currently undergoing an update. The updates include a) the addition of criteria related to the supply chain and governance dimensions of a business/company; and b) the introduction of thresholds (particularly for the leadership and employment criteria) depending on the country and sector.

According to the latest information on the ZX website, alignment with the ZX Criteria can be achieved by:
- Meeting the Basic ZX ESG (including safeguarding requirements)
- Meeting the minimum Governance & Accountability requirements
- AND (a) have at least one of the six ZX Criteria met; and (b) provide a time-bound commitment to meeting an additional Criterion. Thresholds under each Criterion are country and sector specific.

### Basic ZX ESG

#### Criteria as per updates in 2024

**Assessment conducted by Mirva**: Screening and DD to confirm investee is not on the exclusion list, demonstrated commitment to human rights across the supply chain, and no adverse impact regarding gender equality.

**GOVERNANCE AND ACCOUNTABILITY**

At least 3 practices that demonstrate intentional efforts to drive gender equity, representation, and inclusion: (a) implementation of at least 1 Strategic action; (b) implementation of a Gender Action Plan; or (c) application of the MUDT approach as companies are required to develop a Gender Action Plan and its report via disaggregated data.

Percentage of Portfolio that meets the direct criteria: 30 at the beginning of the investment or 50% by the end of the investment’s life

To align with the new criteria, funds must meet the basic 2X ESG (including safeguarding) requirements and minimum Governance & Accountability requirements AND:
- (a) FUND MANAGEMENT LEVEL: meet at least one of the 2X criteria (1–1.5) Or a set time-bound commitment to do so.
- (b) PORTFOLIO LEVEL: meet at least 60% either 30 investors on an origination or 50% by the end of the fund’s life must meet the 2X Criteria

Investment alignment with 2X Criteria can be assessed at different points during the investment life. Mirva will monitor performance of the investee on an annual basis and proactively support companies to meet the 2X ESG criteria, including thresholds in cases where the investor is not meeting a specific requirement. Mirva will agree a formal action plan with the investee (to be included in the Gender Action Plan) to meet the requirement. This approach can also be used for enhancing the alignment of investments in areas where the biggest gaps can be closed, e.g., in the context of the supply chain area. A new or existing investor that is 2X aligned based on Criteria 2 (Leadership) and that demonstrates intentionality to commit to increasing women’s participation in their workforce. The investee would make time-bound commitment to meeting the Employment threshold within 3 or 4 years, showing improvement progress within 2 years.

Mirva will need to comply with France’s Thresholds - Leadership criterion: 40% of female in senior management or in investment committee - Workforce criterion: 50% female employees/workers + one quality indicator.

## 3.4 Investment approach and stages of gender lens integration
The Fund's ESG analysis will be fully integrated within the investment process, from pre-screening, to due diligence, contracting and monitoring of progress. The following steps describe how gender and social inclusion considerations will be integrated at each step of the investment process.

### Pre-Screening: Conduct a country and sector-level assessment using publicly available information and based on Mirova's track record and experience. Here Mirova assesses the potential investment from a ESG perspective against eligibility criteria. The initial ESG review has the purpose to highlight any red flags (e.g., exclusion criteria) as well as the potential positive impacts and risks associated for the investment. This phase generates an ESG & Impact Project Opportunity Note.

### Screening and in-depth analysis: As part of the formal initial ESG assessment, Mirova assesses the potential investors' readiness to comply with the ESG and gender requirements.


---
**Page 132**
---

At this stage, initial questions on the availability of sex disaggregated data, the alignment with MLSF priorities and identified impacts from a gender perspective are discussed.

**Due Diligence:** An in-depth gender assessment focused on understanding a baseline is conducted at this stage and will be completed with the support of the ESG team. During the DD phase, Mirova identified the actions required to meet the Fund's ESG requirements and the investee potential alignment with the ZK criteria. At this stage, Mirova will assess adequacy of existing policies and practices in place for SEAH prevention mechanisms. In line with the ZK Challenge criteria, a company will need to demonstrate to have measures in place to prohibit SEAH, including survivor-centred grievance reporting systems, as well as a gender-sensitive approach to the definition of an impact plan with clear quantitative targets. Technical assistance is provided at this stage to support the investee in translating gender gaps into strategies aligned with their business model and strategy. This will include the development of a Gender Action Plan and the establishment of targets as part of the Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP). Where needed, i.e., in instances where a company does not have satisfactory SEAH prevention policies in place, Mirova will provide necessary action for the development of such policies in line with international standards (such as the IFC Good Practice Note on Managing Contractors' Environmental and Social Performance).

**Contracting:** Mirova and the investee company negotiate the final ESAP and GAP to be included in the investment contract.

**Post-investment:** Investees implement the ESAP commitments and the GAP and report to Mirova. Technical Assistance is provided to eligible and interested organizations to support GAP implementation. Given the importance of SEAH prevention mechanism in the contexts of the investee companies, Mirova will provide technical assistance to companies to ensure that each company is equipped to comply with the expectations on SEAH prevention.

**Monitoring and Reporting:** robust monitoring process to capture qualitative and quantitative indicators and track progress against gender outputs and outcomes. Investees report quarterly on ESAP progress, including the progress on or towards implementation. ESG milestones including gender equality targets will be included in the annual reporting cycle. Companies will report annually on the environmental and social KPIs (see 2.7). Incidents of health and safety, security, Gender Based Violence and harassment (GBVH) are reported promptly within three business days. In line with investee satisfaction criteria, a gender-sensitive approach to grievance mechanism will include a survivor-centred and gender-responsive approach for SEAH-related complaints. Companies will be required to monitor progress and performance on SEAH prevention and report to Mirova on a quarterly basis.

Companies who do not have satisfactory SEAH prevention policies in place at the contracting stage will be expected to develop SEAH prevention policies as a priority action in the implementation of their GAPs.

### 3.5 Gender Technical Assistance
Mirova's experience: the LDN TAF
The LDN TAF could provide grants and repayable grants to (potential) LDN investment projects, with a focus on improving social quality and strengthening environmental and social impacts, strengthen project design and enable the investment project to meet the LDN Fund investment criteria. The TAF also supported projects post-investment to reduce project risks and increase positive social and environmental impacts, as well as to better monitor their impact and provide technical assistance to management more effectively.

The country studies (see section 3.2) indicate that the investees companies will operate in contexts where predominant gender norms, labour force disparities and discrimination over access to resources and assets persist. Hence, investee companies will require gender technical assistance to develop a Gender Action Plan (GAP), including pre-investment support and post-investment support to implement their GAPs and achieve the intended targets of ZK Challenge alignment.

**Gender TA Scope and Approach**
The design of this Gender TAF approach builds on the assumption that all investees will require light touch support to develop their GAP, while only a smaller proportion of the portfolio will require hands-on support to implement their GAP and track progress during project implementation. The TAF will provide technical assistance to help investee companies comply with ESG standards, to enhance their gender and social inclusion impacts, and to align with industry standards while supporting companies to achieve their business and impact results. For these reasons, some activities are envisaged for the whole portfolio.

# Gender Assessment for the Mirva Sustainable Land Fund 2

## Technical Assistance to Mirva Fund Managers - Fund alignment with ZX

Mirva firmly believes that women bring a different perspective and approach to financing, strongly promoting diversity at the level of fund managers and within the wider organisation. Research shows that funds managed by women are more likely to invest in companies that provide access to women entrepreneurs and women investors are more likely to have access to women entrepreneurs' networks. Fund managers require an understanding of the opportunities for enhancing gender inclusion throughout the life cycle of the investment, which is another reason the Mirva SFU integrated gender TA to its fund managers.

The Mirva SFU is committed to ZX compliance both at the portfolio and at the fund level; gender TA will be deployed as part of the implementation of the programme and will:

- Provide an assessment of Mirva's compliance with the ZX Challenge, to identify areas for improvement.
- Develop recommendations for alignment with the ZX criteria, for example, identify strategies to increase the diversity of the workforce, to develop gender inclusive HR policies and practices.
- Deliver capacity building to fund managers and development of investment tools for Mirva SFU fund managers so that they are able to screen investments with a gender lens, to assess the impact of their investments on women's empowerment and to ensure that equity investees in aligning with ZX, and to continuously monitor progress post-investment.

## Impact enhancement assistance (Output 2.2)

The second element of technical assistance under the Mirva SFU will take place in the post-investment phase and will be managed in-house by Mirva. This TA arm will focus on maximising the expected ecological, climate, social, gender and environmental impacts delivered through investments in line with the ZX Challenge criteria to ensure that these are upheld. Mirva SFU will provide different levels of support in terms of portfolio-level awareness to all investees, and tailored 1-1 support for GAP implementation to ensure consistency of reporting. The level of support provided by Mirva SFU will be determined based on the GAP implementation support will include SEAH prevention and GBV prevention capacity building to investee companies, to ensure that all companies funded by Mirva SFU develop and implement SEAH policies and procedures.

## Conclusion

As a result of the pre-investment TA, Mirva SFU investors will have completed gender assessments and will be able to disburse capital.

### 4. Country assessments

The assessment of the eight target countries for the MSLEF identified context-specific challenges for advancing women's participation in forestry and land restoration activities. Some of these countries like Ghana have some of the world’s highest poverty rates. Across all countries, there are disparities between men and women which persist in the areas of economic opportunities, access to education, political participation, and leadership. Particularly in the Sub-Saharan African countries, where dual legal systems persist, statutory laws ensure equal rights and protections for women, gender norms even though customary laws continue to be a major barrier to women’s ability to participate in economic activities. Across Latin American countries, machismo is entrenched, curtailing women's agency and liberty. Further in South Asia, while women's labour force participation has increased over the past decade, they remain underrepresented in land resources and assets across these countries. Labour force participation among women across Latin American and African countries has been rising over the decades. However particularly in agri-food systems, women remain relegated to small-scale value-added work, or in subsistence crops. Some of the barriers faced by women which limit their effective participation in agri-food systems include:

[1] https://www.fao.org/3/x5c060en/cx5c060en.pdf

# Gender Assessment for the Mireva Sustainable Land Fund 2

- Limited access to land as owners, and limited access to required technical inputs and assets
- Concentration in low productivity crops compared to men who are in high productivity or export crop value chains
- Women's participation mostly in manual work like weeding on the fields and in crop export value chains; they mostly conduct administrative and manual tasks like bookkeeping and packing.
- Women often work as family labour and remain unpaid, or paid in kind for their labour, and are not entitled to social security benefits.

In countries like Costa Rica, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Malaysia and Peru women's labour force participation is high, with more than 50% of women working age being engaged in the labour force, with a majority of women engaged in agriculture across value chains of maize, coffee, sweet potatoes, cocoa, etc. Women are also engaged in post-harvest activities such as processing of women in agri-food processing, and post-cultivation activities like harvesting, sorting, drying and carrying the fruit. Women in these countries are also more entrepreneurial; however their enterprises are often small scale and informal. In some cases they are involved in value addition as aggregators, supplying to larger male led enterprises. Some of the more socially conservative countries in the region like Morocco, have a much lower percentage of women's labour force participation (21% in Morocco). Women working across these countries are predominantly engaged in coffee value chains. However, their work in coffee is devalued and women are also often responsible for childcare and household chores. In some cases they are involved in the cultivation of more profitable crops such as rice, fruits, seeds etc. Across Southeast Asian countries, women contribute significantly to both economic and reproductive activities, with labour force participation rate over the 50% mark; women in the Philippines and Malaysia are predominantly engaged in the value addition of rice, rubber, and tea and are registered.

Further, land degradation and deforestation in these countries has severely impacted the quality of lives of women and indigenous communities who depend on forest land for medicinal products, timber, forest produce etc., often leading to conflicts among indigenous communities of Latin America, and Sub-Saharan Africa. Most countries are signatories to or are implementing initiatives towards reforestation, including agroforestry, REDD+ Community Forest Management (CFM) and the Global Plan of Action which aims to address gender disparities by integrating gender considerations throughout all stages of implementation, including conducting gender analyses, implementing capacity-building initiatives for women and men in forest communities, ensuring equal access to resources and establishing gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation mechanisms. This involves understanding and addressing the differentiated roles, needs, and priorities of women and men in forest communities, promoting greater women's participation and ensure their equitable benefit from forest conservation efforts.

# Africa

## Ghana

### Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Ghana, the second most populous country in West Africa, has varied ecosystems, spanning from coastal plains to highlands. The population is predominantly young, with a median age of 20 years old, consisting of 31% young people within the age group 15–24 years.[28] The fertility rate in the country has seen a decline in recent years and stands at 3.5 which is considerably low among the West African countries.

Ghana has a Gender Development Index of 0:946 and the country is classified into "Group 3" countries which have medium equality in terms of disparities between women and men (absolute gender gap index of 18.7% and relative gender gap index of 25.7 percent). A contributor to this deviation is the gender gap in gross national income per capita, with males earning USD USD 4723. On the Gender Inequality Index, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment - Ghana is ranked 130 with an inequality ratio of 0:529.

Gender Assessment for the Mireva Sustainable Land Fund 2

Ghana has ratified various UN conventions focusing on gender equity and inclusion, such as the Convention on Elimination of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1986, and the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990. The country has also ratified The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights, the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa, 2003 (ratified in 2007). The constitution of Ghana, under Article 18(4), provides that the state shall promote equality and freedom of women and men, girls and boys from discrimination based on social or economic status, religion, language, ethnic origin, political opinion, gender, age, disability, sexual orientation, or any other similar condition. The country has also ratified the Optional Protocol to CEDAW.

1. Women's Empowerment: To accelerate efforts and commitments of the government in empowering women (especially women with disability) to have safe and secure livelihood, access to land and resources, and equal participation in decision-making processes, including disparities in education, socio-economic and cultural issues, health and agriculture, trade and related matters.

2. Women's Rights and Access to Justice: To speed up enforcement and domestication of ratified International Treaties, policies and strategies adopted by the Government to tackle violence against women and girls, and to ensure that women have access to justice nationwide.

3. Leadership and Accountable Governance for Women: To support the passage and implementation of an Affirmative Action Law and put in place transformative measures (including leadership development) that will enable women and men participate equally in achieving gender equality and women's empowerment at all levels, including decision making.

4. Gender-sensitive Economic Development: To improve women’s economic opportunities including engendering macro-economic and trade policies so that the basic and strategic needs of both men and women are met; to ensure that women have access to credit and encouragement of a savings culture among women will be enforced.

5. Promoting Gender Roles and Relations: To transform unequal gender relations in rural and urban areas, and to ensure that all levels of government adopt state policies with a gender and women’s equity lens to ensure compliance.

Gender Assessment for the Mireva Sustainable Land Fund 2

Violence against women is still heavily prevalent in Ghana and between 2015 to 2018, a total of 63,420 cases were reported to the country’s Domestic Violence and Victim Support Unit with physical abuse being the most reported form of violence and close to 85% victims/ survivors being women. To protect women from domestic violence, Ghana has enacted the Criminal Code Act and the Women’s Rights Act defining acts of domestic violence, provisions and process for protection. Widows among Ghana’s ethnic groups, Akan and Ewe, have to face humiliating widow rice which the country’s legislation is trying to address by enacting the Widow Rice Prohibition Act (2019). This is a practice that is prevalent along with statutory law in Ghana. About 52% of the country’s population practices polygamy and this has led to women being married off at an early age, which is against the law. The practice of matrilineal inheritance which is more flexible allowing for both men and women to inherit land, however, has been found to be a major factor in leading to infertile land than in women systems and hence end up more likely to be managed farms.

Ghana has yet to implement any affirmative action policy setting targets or quotas around women’s representation at all levels of government, with a bill still awaiting approval by the country’s parliament as of 2021. The number of women in Parliament increased from 3% in 2008 to 9% in 2020; however, it still remains significantly lower than the targets set under this policy. In 2012, there were 1.1% female representation at the local government level and 4.5% at the regional level. On the other hand, there has been a decline in the number of women representatives elected in the District Assembly from 37 to 29 between 2008-2016. The country’s electoral system is also biased with a total of 909 women candidates. Women face entry barriers such as limited resources for financing campaigns, gender based violence, and lack of support from their families along with lack of political will by the government to ensure that women are elected into public office. These factors coupled with crunch, corruption, ignorance, and discrimination also prevented women from contesting again.

Women’s health

The Maternal Mortality Ratio in Ghana stands at 308 per 100,000 live births. Compared to other sub-Saharan African countries, Ghana has a high awareness of modern contraception among women; with 95% men and women being aware of modern contraception and 23% married women in Ghana have an unmet need for family planning. While the prevalence of HIV among women is low in Ghana (i.e., <1%), there are still some challenges that prevent women from accessing voluntary counseling and testing towards those with HIV, according to Ghana’s Demographic and Health Survey (2022).

Women's education

Over a decade from 2010 to 2020, women’s literacy rate in Ghana has increased by over 10 percentage points, from 65% to 76%. To ensure universal education, the government of Ghana is implementing Free Compulsory Universal Basic Education policy, as well as the Free Secondary School Education and Free Senior High School Education policies. At the primary level, completion rates for both boys and girls are almost at par, around 94%. At the secondary lower level, the completion rates among boys and girls are almost at par, at around 74%. Further, at the basic education level, the completion rate for girls is slightly higher than that of boys. The government of Ghana has supplemented efforts by launching initiatives such as the Ghana School Feeding Programme (GSFP) to ensure that children have access to food while in school. The GSFP also provides child education officers to work with partners and related agencies. To increase girls’ participation in STEM, Girls in Science and Technology (GIST) is a government initiative that has been rolled out in 10 districts of the country, where girls are made aware of various STEM-related courses available for them to study, and career prospects in these fields. Girls also have an opportunity to interact with young women who are already working in STEM careers.

The Ministry of Communications and Digitalisation (MoCid) has launched a School for Ladies in Stem Initiative with an objective to increase the number of female students pursuing STEM-related courses from 10% in 2015 to by 31% by 2031 by providing women equal opportunities to pursue and thrive in STEM careers, narrowing the gender gap, and preventing biases in the products and services produced by private sector STEM companies.

Women’s economic participation and agricultural work

Women's labor force participation is high in Ghana at 67%. Further entrepreneurship among women in Ghana is highly prevalent and widely accepted. Mastercard's 2021 Index of Women Entrepreneurs for the third consecutive report year found Ghana to be one of three countries with the highest scores.

percentage of women business owners globally, at 37.2%. However a majority of women-run businesses remain micro-enterprises (80%). Given being an agrarian economy, 46% of the female labor force is engaged in primary production agriculture. producing 70% of the country's subsistence crops, yet they are constrained by lack of access to credit and other forms of formal finance which limit their agricultural productivity. Only 38% of the total agricultural land holders are women, and 88% of the large farm land (>5 acres) is cultivated by men. Cultivation of certain crops like groundnuts and cocoa is also dominated by male farmers.

While most agri producers in Ghana are small scale farmers, women predominantly take up the role of aggregators, buying produce from the North of the country and re-selling to large wholesalers in the South. Women traders in the North of Ghana, who buy produce directly from smallholder farmers in the county are usually women, however they buy their product at a small-scale from wholesalers and sell it on to larger scale traders. This is due to limited capital and storage facilities available to them. Women traders in Ghanaian markets also include “market queens” who are democratically elected leaders of traditional self-help women traders groups.

Land Degradation and Gender

Rural women in Ghana often depend on forest produce for survival and are involved as active collectors of forest products such as firewood, collection, sale in the market and for extraction of medicinal resources, for ritualistic practices. Forest degradation, with a net annual deforestation rate at 3.51%, has an adverse impact on women whose livelihood depends on forest produce. For instance, women who harvest non-timber forest products (NTFPs) are increasingly facing competition from charcoal producers and Rosewood being illegally harvested and exported. FAO’s Forest and Landscape Restoration (FLR) project in Ghana, together with the Community Forestry Trust (CFT), are collaborating to work in baobab and shea value chains, aiming to address this issue by discouraging cutting down of baobab and shea trees for their charcoal and wood products and providing communities with alternate ways of using these resources.

# Costa Rica

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Costa Rica is a Central American country with a huge rainforest cover and a population of 5.2 million people, 22% of whom are in the age group 10-24 years. The fertility rate in the country has been declining since 1970.

Costa Rica has a Gender Development Index of 0.996 and the country is classified with "Group 1" countries which means the country has high equality in HDI achievements between women and men (although there are still gender gaps). Women's earnings in Costa Rica are 58% lower than men's, women earn close to 1.4 times more than men, with men paid an average of $23,760 & women paid $15,892 per year. The country has a high level of gender equality in education and employment, and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment. Costa Rica is ranked 80 out of 146 countries.

Gender Assessment for the Mireva Sustainable Land Fund 2

Costa Rica has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1986 along with the optional protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women, and indicates that its country's commitment to ensuring equality between men and women is reflected in its national legislation. Costa Rica has also been implementing commitments under the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, making strides in its efforts to ensure gender equality and the empowerment of women.

Socio-cultural context and political participation

In traditional Costa Rican society, patriarchy has historically played a significant role in shaping gender relations. Women have faced discrimination in rural areas and among indigenous or Afro-descendant face violence, and abuse in both domestic spheres and the world of work. In 2007 the National Survey on Sexual and Reproductive Health (2016) revealed that 35.1% of women admit to having experienced sexual violence.

Despite progress in recent years, women in Costa Rica still face challenges in political representation and leadership roles. In 1996, voluntary party quotas were introduced in the country and amended many times since then. The law requires that at least one-third of candidates on electoral lists should be women. In 2022, Costa Rica elected 47.4% women to its single house of parliament.

The Global Gender Gap Report (2021) indicates that Costa Rica has made progress in the past decade. However, women continue to face barriers to political participation, particularly digital violence and denigration on social media. The country became the first in the region to promote affirmative action for women in politics.

action in municipal level elections in 2010 municipal elections. However the position of mayor is usually held by men and women women elected as deputy mayors. In 2016 only 13% of mayors' seats were occupied by women.

## Women's health

Costa Rica has made strides in maternal health compared to many other countries in the region. According to UNICEF, Costa Rica's maternal mortality ratio stood at 57 deaths per 100,000 live births. This figure indicates a relatively low rate compared to the global average and demonstrates the effectiveness of the country’s healthcare system. However, only about half (49%) of pregnant women in the country receive prenatal care. 14% of women in Costa Rica also have an unmet need for family planning.

## Women's education

Costa Rica has made strides in women's education. 53.8% of women in Costa Rica have at least some secondary education, compared to 52.3% of men and 60.2% complete secondary school, compared to 49.7% of men. The country also boasts a high number of female students enrolled in STEM courses with 35.4% of Engineering courses are women, however there is evidence of a gender gap in ICT course enrollment. In addition, the government has made efforts to increase the participation and retention of girls through initiatives such as "Deportes para las niñas" (Sports for Girls) which seeks to engage girls about financial and non-financial returns of studying STEM, and it's impact on society.

## Women's economic participation and agricultural work

In Costa Rica, women’s labor force participation is at 57%. This indicates that a growing proportion of women are actively engaged in the labor market, contributing to the country's economic development and growth. The services sector is one of the major employers for women in Costa Rica, with women

working in industries like education, commerce, hotels and restaurants, and communication (n=46). Women are well-represented in service-oriented roles as professional and technical workers (48%). 10 As such, the country's agriculture census in 2014, women were found to hold only a 9.1% of the farm area in the country, and 13% of total number of farms. 10 In farmed owned and run by women, agricultural tasks are mainly related with livestock farming (58%), followed by horticulture (27%) and production of by-products; rural tourism, and waste treatment. Women engaged in the agriculture sector are mostly involved in small-scale activities, such as subsistence farming (40%), commercial livestock farming (29%). 10 Fruit processing for export in Costa Rica employs female labor for harvesting, sorting, packaging, and distribution. The Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Irrigation, Equality and Social Inclusion for the Costa Rican Agricultural and Rural Sector 2020-2030 led by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MINAE), with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the Inter-American Institute for Cooperation on Agriculture (IICA) and UNDP Costa Rica, to ensure gender equality in rural areas. The project aims at increasing women's participation in agriculture with opportunities. The effort has involved assessment of needs of women form 8 regions in the country, and revised the National Rural Development Plan (PNDR) to include measures for financing for productive projects, marketing spaces, and infrastructure to ensure agro productivity.

Land Degradation and Gender

Costa Rica has made remarkable strides in reversing deforestation and land degradation. According to the World Bank, Costa Rica's forest cover has doubled its tropical forests in just a few decades, with forest cover increasing from around 26% in the 1980s to over 52% today. This substantial increase in forested area is due to several factors including government policies and programs, international development initiatives and the establishment of protected areas and ambitious reforestation targets and innovative policies such as the Payment for Environmental Services (PES) program. The restoration of degraded forests in Costa Rica has had positive impacts on women, as they are often disproportionately affected by environmental degradation. Women have been involved in the process of restoring and maintaining forest ecosystems and implementing sustainable land management practices. Costa Rica has created opportunities for women to participate in conservation efforts, access ecotourism and agroforestry.

# Peru Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Peru is a country in South America that is covered in part by the Amazon rainforest. The country has a population of 34.4 million people, 25% of whom are in the age group 10-24 years and the average fertility rate is 1.9 per woman.

Peru has a Gender Development Index (GDI) of 0.655 and the country is classified as "Group 2" countries that have made significant but not fully equal progress in HDI achievements between women and men (i.e., absolute deviation between 2.5 percent and 5 percent). The country's mean years of schooling for girls is lower than that of boys, with a ratio of 0.83:1 compared to 1.47:1 for boys per capita in the country. Women earn about $690 annually, which is significantly lower than men's earnings of $2,500 annually. On the Gender Inequality Index (GII), which measures gender gaps across three dimensions—health, education, and economic opportunities, health, and empowerment, Peru is ranked 90 with an inequality ratio of 0.380.

### Legal status of women

Peru has ratified all major UN conventions to solidify their commitment to gender equality, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and its Protocol in 1985. Peru is also a signatory to the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, which affirms the rights of indigenous women and has ratified the UN Declaration on Indigenous Women adopted in 2012.

Regional laws have been enacted to address gender equality issues such as Gender-Based Violence and Eradicate Violence against Women – Convention Belm Do Park. And internationally Peru has adapted the Beijing Declaration and Platform for at the Fourth World Conference on Women in 1995, is included in the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Paris Agreement.

Peru's Constitution includes several articles that guarantee equal rights to women, affirming the country’s commitment to gender equality and women’s empowerment. Among these provisions are Article 2, which prohibits discrimination on any basis including sex or gender; Article 3, which prohibits gender-based violence in marriage and other relationships; and Article 4, which protects women's rights in marriage and family life from being affected by gender or any other factor. Furthermore, Article 4 ensures the protection of women’s rights in marriage and family life from being affected by gender or any other factor.

The constitution also guarantees that the composition of local government bodies is determined by law to ensure representation of women in all levels of government. The LAG region which has a high score on World Bank's Women, Business and Law Index, scoring 95 out of 100, has taken steps to address gender equality issues such as Gender-Based Violence and Eradicate Violence against Women – Convention Belm Do Park.

Additionally, Peru has a National Plan on Gender Equality 2017-2018 aimed at addressing gender disparities and promoting women's empowerment. The plan focuses on four main areas: education, health, economic empowerment through access to education, employment, and entrepreneurship opportunities. Additionally, the plan aims to prevent and eradicate gender-based violence, promote women’s rights in marriage and family life from being affected by gender or any other factor.

# Socio-cultural context and political participation

Women in Peru face a double burden of work where traditionally defined roles of women as caregivers is reinforced by their economic dependence on men. Full citizenship for women is the dream of only the most marginalized.149 Migrant populations of Venezuelan women in the country are also particularly vulnerable to being forced into transactional sex work, along with other forms of abuse and exploitation.150 Women in Peru have a high risk of violence against them. According to UNICEF, Peru has one of Latin America's highest rates of violence against women, with 6 or 7 of ten women suffering violence at some point during their lives.151 Violence is also experienced by girls and boys. Girls are more often perpetuated by women on their boys and girls.152 Violence is so internalized in the country that 59% of the population believes that it is normal for a husband to beat his wife.153 The government has been aware of this issue, as the country's government has been paying keen attention to its, as 14% of the women have been married before age 18. The government has passed an amendment to the country's marriage law to prohibit and eliminate early marriages.154

### Women’s health

Peru has shown significant and quick progress when it comes to improvement of maternal health services to women, and maternal mortality rates have dropped significantly. 94% births in 2016 were attended by skilled personnel compared to only 60% in 2005.[^3] However abortion is still a crime in the country and is only allowed for reproductive health up to 22 weeks old, which is restrictive as women’s agency is a country where in 2021, 52.6% women reported that their pregnancies were unwanted at the time of conception or in the past years.[^4]

Gender Assessment for the Mivira Sustainable Land Fund 2

Women in Peru are concentrated in the cocoa and coffee value chain, where they undertake tasks like harvesting, fertilizing, grafting, weeding, pruning and picking cocoa fruits. Further, since these activities are considered less strenuous, women also simultaneously work in other crop value chains like rice, palm, and banana. In addition to this, women in Peru are more likely than men to undertake more commercially oriented tasks in these value chains, conducting sales and marketing.124

Across indigenous communities, women conduct activities in family or community gardens; they also raise livestock, engage in agroforestry, and non-timber forest products while men do hunting, clear land for agriculture, and manage the household.125

Land Degradation and Gender

Close to 87% of land in Peru is indigenous and community land.126 Peat swamps in the country are also being degraded.127 Land degradation in Peru is driven by various factors, including illegal mining, unsustainable extraction of natural resources, and the concentration of land in private hands. Illegal mining activities cause soil erosion, deforestation, loss of biodiversity, and water pollution. Deforestation leads to soil erosion, and water contamination, exacerbating land degradation. Unsustainable extraction of timber, minerals, and other forest products also contributes to land degradation by reducing vegetation cover, further deteriorating land quality. Additionally, the concentration of land in the hands of large landholders and corporate entities has led to deforestation, loss of biodiversity, and soil erosion, which have negatively impacted tenure insecurity and marginalization. These factors, combined with inadequate land governance and weak enforcement of environmental regulations, contribute to widespread land degradation in Peru, posing threats to food security, water quality, and the environment.

Deforestation and land degradation can have grave impact on women in these communities. Communities are currently at the forefront of the passing of a 2018 law, which mandates 30% representation of women in peasant communities.128, 129

# South East Asia

## Philippines

### Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

The Philippines is an archipelagic country in Southeast Asia, in the western Pacific Ocean, composed of over 7000 islands, of which approximately 2000 are inhabited. The total population of 117.3 million people, 28% of whom are in the age group 10-24 years. The fertility rate in the country has declined by almost half since 1965.

The Philippines has a Gender Development Index (GDI) of 0.990 and the country is classified with "Group 1" countries which means that the country has high equality in HDI achievements between women and men (calculated as the average of the GDI, the gender inequality index (GII), and the sex-disaggregated HDI). The life expectancy at birth for girls aged 0-5 years is slightly higher than that of boys, at 9.2 years compared to 8.7 years for boys. However, the average earnings of women is significantly lower than that of men: $487 per year compared to $1311 per capita for men. On the Gender Inequality Index (GII), the Philippines ranks 5th in Asia and 62nd globally on issues such as education, reproductive health, and empowerment, with an inequality ratio of 0.419.

### Legal status of women

The Philippines has signed key UN conventions related to gender equality, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1981 and its Optional Protocol in 2003 and the Convention on Rights of the Child. The country is also a signatory to the Beijing Platform for Action, the Program of Action of the International Conference on Population and Development, and the World Programme of Action concerning Youth with Special Reference to Africa.

The Philippine Constitution contains several articles and provisions that protect the rights of women. Article IX, Section 14 guarantees equal opportunities in education, particularly for girls. Section 15 ensures equal representation of women in government positions. Section 16 mandates the state to address the needs of women, particularly those in marginalized sectors.

Article XIII, Section 17 underscores the protection of women's rights in the workplace, including equal employment opportunities and benefits, and special assistance for women during pregnancy. Article XVII, Section 24 guarantees equal representation of women in decision-making positions. The Philippines also has a Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment (GEWE) Framework to promote gender equality, ensure good governance, ensure gender-responsive legislation and policy which address gender-based discrimination, and improve the lives of women through education, health, employment, entrepreneurship, and livelihood programs, as well as promoting equal pay for work, enhance women's participation in decision-making positions, and strengthen women’s representation in all sectors including politics, business, and civil society and improve gender-responsive social protection and services focusing on enhancing access to quality social services including healthcare, education, and social welfare. The GEWE Framework also aims at ensuring the full participation of women and strengthen gender research through collection of gender-disaggregated data to inform evidence-based policy making and planning in order to promote gender equality.

The Magna Carta of Women (MCW) enacted as Republic Act 9715, it outlines provisions for the protection and promotion of women's rights. The MCW covers areas including education, employment, health, and political participation. The MCW emphasizes the protection and promotion of women’s rights, particularly those belonging to marginalized sectors of society. It also aims at ensuring that women are free from all forms of discrimination and violence against women, while also promoting their economic empowerment and development.

### Socio-cultural context and political participation

Paternalistic norms have traditionally constrained Filipino women’s empowerment, perpetuating inequalities in various aspects of life such as education, employment, and decision-making. These norms often place women in a subordinate position within families and communities, limiting their economic independence and social mobility. Fruits from families in Philippines (cf.) however, have led to women’s increased participation in the labor force, with more women entering or remaining active in the formal economy.

Gender Voter Voice is low in the Philippines, for the latest 2012 National Demographic and Health Survey (NDHS) 17.5% of Filipino women aged 18-49 have experienced some type of physical, sexual, and emotional violence from intimate partners.

The country has a huge gender gap in political participation with only 27.3% parliamentarians being women as of March 2023. The constitution of the country recognises the importance of having women engage in nation building and guarantees equal rights to participate in all levels of government, including local government units. However, discriminatory practices within political parties, such as tokenism and exclusion from decision-making processes, also impede women’s advancement in politics.

The Philippines has a low score on the World Economic Forum’s (WEF) 2023 Global Gender Gap Report. The case for gender quotas and temporary special measures is discussed in the document.

# Women's health

## Maternal mortality ratio in the country is 121 per 100,000 live births (14). The country is implementing a conditional cash transfer program for pregnant women to reduce maternal mortality and improve access to modern contraception. This program provides financial incentives of $50 for each trimester of pregnancy, with which pregnant women receive cash transfers when they complete prenatal care beginning in the first trimester of their pregnancy. The program also includes free transportation to health facilities, free public cars and pants, and attend parent effectiveness service classes. This program has particularly been instrumental in achieving a reduction in stunting (16). However, women's access to quality health services is limited by the distribution of skilled birth attendants and other reproductive health professionals, who are mostly concentrated in urban areas. A few years ago the country launched the Universal Health Coverage Program (UHC), which aims at providing universal coverage for all citizens. Each beneficiary is matched with a primary healthcare team, and includes access to reproductive health services. However, women's access to quality health services remains limited by the distribution of skilled birth attendants and other reproductive health professionals who are mostly concentrated in urban areas.

### Women’s education

When it comes to education, women in the Philippines are at par with men. The country has achieved gender parity in primary education, however the gap appears in secondary (94.18% enrollment rate for boys vs. 75.23% for girls) and tertiary levels (60.5% of female students enrolled in higher education compared to 65.5% of male students). As a result, women are less likely than men to have completed secondary or higher education levels. The country has a Gender-Responsive Basic Education Policy that outlines comprehensive strategies to address gender disparities and promote equal access to learning opportunities, fair treatment, and equitable outcomes for girls and boys. It aims to protect children from gender-based violence, discrimination, and bullying in schools, address gender-based barriers, reduce gender gaps in education, and ensure the quality of education through inclusive curriculum, teaching methodologies, and support services to empower and transform gender relations.

Higher education is also highly gendered in the country, with women opting for courses which are considered more feminine. Women make up 58% of graduates from social sciences programs but only 24.5% of graduates from engineering courses in the country, while in health and welfare related courses women make up 71.9% of the graduates.

Social stereotypes and biases, lack of female role models and mentors, limited access to resources, and gender-based discrimination in academic and professional settings, contribute to the underrepresentation of women in STEM disciplines.
