Only 48% of Filipino women are in the labor force, which is one of the lowest in the EAP region.107-110 Similar to women developing in education, in economically oriented activities, women majorly work in sectors that mirror their reproductive roles, for instance domestic work and health and education fields, while men dominate in non-reproductive roles such as agriculture and construction. Women earn lower wages, with daily wage for men being 50% higher than that of women.111 Labour migration is a pervasive issue in the Philippines, and migration of women to the Middle East and South East Asian countries has been increasing over time.112 The majority of female migrants are domestic helpers or workers in foreign countries.113 Women entrepreneurs in the country are concentrated in the MSME sector, with 60% of women-owned enterprises being in this category.114 These include food processing and manufacturing enterprises, followed by service-oriented enterprises (56%). Only however only 4.4% agric enterprises are women-led.115

In the Philippines, agriculture serves as a significant source of employment, engaging around 11 million Filipinos, which accounts for 27 percent of the country's total employment as of 2016. Both men and women participate in the agricultural sector, with male agricultural workers constituting 32.5 percent of total male employment and female agricultural workers accounting for 48.9% of total female employment in 2018. Women are involved in crucial roles in food production, including harvesting, processing, and marketing of agricultural products such as rice, corn, and fruits.116 Women also engage in preparing fishponds/cages, collecting fingerlings, feeding, monitoring, managing accounts, processing, marketing, and selling of aquatic products. While the Maguindanao women's group has sought to address gender disparities in land ownership by procuring land legally in property titles, these gaps persist, with only 10% of all landowners being women.117 This contributes to women’s economic vulnerability as they are unable to access credit and other financial services for agricultural production and marketing.

Land Degradation and Gender

Land degradation in the Philippines poses significant challenges, particularly for women. Land degradation reduces the livelihoods of many Filipino women who are heavily reliant on agriculture and natural resources for their income and sustenance. Women often bear the brunt of environmental degradation and climate change impacts, which exacerbate existing gender inequalities and increase climate-related risks.10 The Philippines Biodiversity Strategic Action Plan (PBSP)2016-2025 promotes gender as a fundamental principle in biodiversity conservation and sustainable use of natural resources. The PBSP is aligned with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) Strategic Plan for Biodiversity 2011-2020 and the 20 Achiv Biodiversity Targets, comprehensively incorporating gender equality into its framework.

A corresponding Department Administrative Order No 2016-35 mandates the adoption of the PBSP 2015-2025, assigning the Biodiversity Management Bureau (BMB) of the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR) to coordinate with other government agencies, local governments, non-government entities, financial institutions, and educational institutions. Although the PBSP vision does not explicitly mention gender equality, it is reflected in the BMB's Gender Equality Action Plan 2015-2024, which is considered the minimum standard for planning, implementing, monitoring, and evaluating all programs, projects, and activities outlined in the PBSP document. Additionally, gender-specific indicators are incorporated into each program intervention where appropriate.11

Malaysia

Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Malaysia is a country in Southeast Asia with a total population of 30.3 million people, 23% of whom are in the age group 15-64 years. The fertility rate for Malaysia has steadily declined significantly since the 1990s and is currently 1.8 per woman (11).12

Malaysia has a Gender Development Index of 0.582, and the country is classified with "Group I" countries that have a gender gap in SDG indicators of less than or equal to 30% HDI achievements between women and men (absolute deviation less than 2.5 percent). While the mean years of education for women and men are close to par, the gap is driven by gender disparity in earnings, with the average earnings of women being 70% that of men.13 The country has a high female labor force participation rate of 64%, but measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and

### Legal status of women
Malaysia has ratified the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women in 1985; however not its optional protocol. Demonstrating its commitment to reducing the gender wage gap, the country also ratified the Convention 100 and the Minimum Wage Fixing Convention (No.26) in 1973. The government has taken measures to ensure equal remuneration for men and women. Regionally, the country has also ratified the Beijing Platform for Action, Commitments and National Strategies for Women's Empowerment. Malaysia has taken proactive measures to mainstream gender equality across ministries. This includes the establishment of a Gender Unit in the Prime Minister’s Office and the inclusion of gender into policies, programs, and initiatives. Additionally, the government has adopted various measures such as gender budgeting (with the Ministry of Women Family and Community Development producing the Manual on Gender Budgeting in Malaysia), gender impact assessments, and gender-responsive

The country performs relatively well in areas related to women’s economic empowerment such as Entrepreneurship and Pension related laws (75/100), with women having the right sign-contracts and register business names in their own name. However, Malaysia scores poorly on other areas where the country scores a 0 on Parenthood-related laws, and very poorly on marriage law (40/100) as women cannot obtain divorce the same way as men, and paternity leave benefits are not available to women.[20]

### Socio-cultural context and political participation

Social norms in Malaysia often impose limitations on women. These norms include traditional gender roles that assign women primarily to domestic duties and caregiving responsibilities, while men are expected to be breadwinners. Women face various legal restrictions and social taboos specifically such as the requirement for women to wear a covered headress (hijab) and the legal recognition of polygamy. These norms can limit women’s access to education, economic opportunities, employment, and political participation.[21][22] Violence against women is a pervasive issue in Malaysia; incidence of which saw a 42% increase in 2021, from 5,260 to 7,489 cases as reported by the Ministry of Women, Family and Community Development. and girls. [10] The Domestic Violence Act (DVA) of Malaysia, enacted in 1994, serves as a legislative framework to address domestic violence and protect survivors. This Act was later amended in 2017 to strengthen its provisions and enhance protection for survivors. The amendment expanded the definition of domestic violence to include emotional abuse and financial control. It also introduced measures promoting a comprehensive approach to addressing different forms of violence within the home. Additionally, the amendment introduced measures to improve access to protection orders, support services, and legal remedies for women.

There is a huge gender gap in political participation of women in the country, with women representing only 21% of parliamentarians and holding 20-69% of ministerial roles.[11] The government has launched several initiatives aimed at increasing women's representation. These include the appointment of Firda Wandra Putra (Women in Politics Fellowship) to provide young women with exposure to public life as leaders, and the establishment of a Women's Parliamentary Caucus to promote gender equality and increase women's representation in politics and decision-making positions. These include the 30% quota for women in local council appointments and the 30% quota for women in political party leadership positions.[12] However, despite these efforts, women continue to face significant barriers to political participation including limited access to financial resources and campaign support, as well as societal norms that discourage women from running and attempt systemic measures such as harassment and derogatory remarks against other women parliamentarians within parliament.

### Women’s health

The maternal mortality rate in Malaysia is 29 per 100,000 live births which is much lower than other ASEAN countries. The country has a Family Health Programme which was launched in 1998 and free antenatal and postnatal services are provided at public facilities. As per the latest National Health and Morbidity Survey in the country conducted in 2022, 98.4% of births in the country are attended by skilled health personnel. However, only 65.1% of women aged 15–49 years old had received at least 4 visits for pregnancy care. However, when it comes to unplanned pregnancies and unsafe abortions, the situation is not as good. In 2022, 38.7% of married or cohabiting women had a need for family planning methods. Further, the use of modern contraception methods is very low, compared with other countries in Southeast Asia. The main reasons for this are lack of access to family planning services and limited knowledge about family planning. Areas for this low usage include casual labour, surrounding contractions, limited access to comprehensive reproductive health services, and religious beliefs influencing contraceptive decision-making.

### Women’s education

Under Article 12(1) of the Constitution of Malaysia, girls and boys have equal rights to education. Girls and boys in primary school are almost at par in education enrolment, with 98.3% enrollment for boys and 97.2% for girls in primary schooling. 86.1% enrolment rate for boys and 82% for girls, however the gender gap is more pronounced in secondary schools where only 50.4% of girls are enrolled compared to 53.84% of boys. While technical and vocational education and training (TVET) traditionally draws more male students, female enrolment in Polytechnics and Community Colleges is notable. Women contribute significantly to the country’s workforce.

## Women's economic participation and agricultural work

Labor force participation rate in the country among women is 56%. In the Rate of activity (Rate being out of employment) among women, it is 48.7% compared to men at 63.9%, more than one fifth less for women compared to 10.9% for men. In Malaysia has been struggling with a phenomenon of "leaking" talent among women, who quit the workforce after having their first child. When it comes to distribution by sector, women are predominantly distributed in sectors like education and health (38%), while more than half of them are concentrated in agriculture and related activities (57%). This also reflects a positive trend, wherein more and more women are completing tertiary education and entering the workforce, which is expected to help in achieving gender parity and balance between urban, educated women. The Global Gender Gap data report also reflects a narrowing gender gap w.r.t employment in Professional and technical work, with 54% men compared to 45.97% women.

In Malaysia, women play a significant role in both the agricultural sector and the agro-based industry. In agriculture, approximately 18% of farmers are women, actively involved in various cultivation tasks such as

In the agro-based industry, women's involvement is even more prominent, as they are responsible for processing agricultural produce into value-added products such as foodstuffs, and fruit-based products. To support women in this sector, the Malaysian government has introduced initiatives such as the Danian financing program by the Council of Trust (MARAK) and facilitated over 11,000 women to access microfinance loans worth RM25 million (approximately USD6.7 million). These initiatives aim to encourage women's participation in entrepreneurship and enhance their competitiveness within the agro-based industry. The Ministry of Women, Family and Community Development (MWFCD) has prioritized talent development to promote inclusivity and gender equality for women in the agro-based sector as part of its National Action Plan on Gender Equality Policy 2021-2030 (NAP 2.0). NAP 2.0 aims to facilitate women's participation in the agrobased sector by providing training and knowledge transfer on essential skills needed for the industry.[17,18]

### Land Degradation and Gender

In Malaysia, various forms of land degradation, including soil erosion, deforestation, and loss of biodiversity, are significant environmental challenges. These environmental changes have generated impacts, particularly on women and indigenous peoples. For women, who often play significant roles in agriculture and natural resource management, these changes can lead to reduced access to resources, decreased income, and increased workload due to the need for soil conservation measures. Indigenous communities, such as the Bataks (that are engaged in maritime-related activities) are particularly vulnerable to land degradation because of their reliance on coastal ecosystems.[19]

Deforestation and habitat destruction can disrupt their traditional way of life, affecting access to food, water, and other resources essential for their livelihoods. Efforts have been made to address these issues by promoting sustainable land management practices and empowering women and indigenous communities to participate in conservation initiatives.[20-24]

# Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## IMPACT STATEMENT:
If the appropriate investment capital and technical assistance are provided to MSLF:

- 2 staff and investees, THEN THEY will be able to engage women in their workforce and as beneficiaries in the supply chains contributing to women's economic empowerment and to socially-inclusive production models. BECAUSE companies will be adequately incentivized and capacitated to operate and to align with global gender equality commitments.

## OUTPUT STATEMENT:
Finance and technical assistance lead to increased capacity of MSLF investees to engage women beneficiaries, to hire and retain women employees, and create gender inclusive workplaces for women to thrive.

### MSLF2 Gender Objectives:

- Reach women beneficiaries and involve women at all levels of the workforce and in value chains
- Ensure high ESG standards and compliance, including gender-related risk identification and mitigation
- Support investee companies to align with industry standards and influence portfolio to adopt gender inclusive business practices
- Align with the Charter for Women 50% off the MSLF2 portfolios to be 2X aligned
- Generate and disseminate knowledge about best practices for women's engagement in forestry, sustainable agriculture and land restoration activities across geographies
